Adversarial Verification

15 Adversarial Verification

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why can a machine pass its normal operating tests and still fail when its protective response is needed?

The claims that matter most in a physical machine describe what it does after something has already gone wrong, when the learned proposer stalls, an observation arrives old, a message is lost, or the supply sags while a loaded body moves toward a person. Normal operation almost never exercises those responses, so thousands of clean runs can coexist with a stopping mechanism that has never once been made to stop the machine. Without deliberate testing, the first real trial of that mechanism happens in service, where a late response delivers energy that no software correction can recall. Establishing such a claim requires creating the fault on purpose, measuring the physical response with instruments the fault cannot reach, and scoring it against acceptance conditions fixed before the test. Every condition left untested still limits what the result can support. Verification makes those limits explicit before release grants any action authority to cross the causal boundary.

Learning Objectives
  • Derive test conditions and stopping oracles from the machine’s operating envelope
  • Derive faults from design assumptions, physical hazards, authority-transfer failures, and attacks on the proposer and sensors
  • Select qualification environments that exercise the causal mechanism behind each claim
  • Design hardware injections with independent witnesses and precommitted acceptance conditions
  • Interpret temporal safety margins and search for disturbances that violate them
  • Evaluate the assumptions limiting simulation transfer, rare-event estimates, and formal proofs
  • Assemble fault records that preserve failed, invalid, unobservable, and untested cases
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

15.1 The Claim a Fault Trial Must Test

The authority record at the close of Supervisory Intervention can log weeks of clean handovers and still say nothing about what the machine does when a duplicate writer appears on the bus, a stale packet replays, or a heartbeat drops. None of those faults occurred during normal service. Every preceding chapter of this book rests on protective claims of the same kind: a chunk lease that expires within its declared deadline, a permission path that halts the mobile base within its clear distance, or a safety microcontroller that outlasts a voltage brownout on the shared electrical supply. Because those fallback paths stay dormant until a subsystem breaks, an unexpected fault discovered in service has already crossed the causal boundary into the room, releasing kinetic energy that no software update can undo. Verification turns these design assertions into auditable evidence by provoking each fault deliberately before release, under the physical loads that make the protective response hardest to execute.

A trial passes only when an independent sensor witnesses the act.

Policy evaluation measures how capably a learned candidate policy completes tasks across declared environments. Verification, by contrast, tests the integrated machine as a whole: proposer, runtime enforcer, communication buses, power distribution network, and physical body. High task completion rates during policy evaluation do not show that an independent emergency stop engages when the neural proposer hangs; conversely, a reliable emergency stop does not show that the learned policy navigates aisles smoothly. Qualifying an embodied machine requires both kinds of evidence gathered on the same production hardware and software stack. The same distinction separates raw policy autonomy from whole-machine safety, because an enforcer applying a least-change correction can keep the robot safe during a mission while isolating and logging a hazardous proposal from the model.

⇄ Contrast: Policy evaluation, which scores the learned proposer’s task proficiency rather than the whole machine’s protective responses, is established in Closed-Loop Evaluation.

1 Digital Twin: Grieves and Vickers (2017) trace the concept to a 2002 presentation on product lifecycle management, and Glaessgen and Stargel (2012) gave it the sense aerospace adopted, a simulation of one as-built vehicle, updated continually from that vehicle’s own sensors and history so that it mirrors the life of its flying twin. The original meaning therefore depended on continual calibration against one particular physical article. A simulator that has never been compared with a measured stop of this machine is a model, not a twin, and treating it as a twin is the delusion this chapter names.

Grieves, Michael, and John Vickers. 2017. “Digital Twin: Mitigating Unpredictable, Undesirable Emergent Behavior in Complex Systems.” In Transdisciplinary Perspectives on Complex Systems, edited by Franz-Josef Kahlen, Shannon Flumerfelt, and Anabela Alves. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-38756-7_4.
Glaessgen, Edward, and David Stargel. 2012. “The Digital Twin Paradigm for Future NASA and U.S. Air Force Vehicles.” 53rd AIAA/ASME/ASCE/AHS/ASC Structures, Structural Dynamics and Materials Conference. https://doi.org/10.2514/6.2012-1818.

Treating passing assertions in a software simulator as sufficient proof of physical safety is the digital twin delusion.1 A physics engine or digital emulator models timing jitter, power transients, and floor traction only to the extent that its authors anticipate and calibrate those effects; a simulated controller cannot fail on an unmodeled supply sag or bus collision. Passive operational logs invite an equally dangerous complacency: millions of incident-free operating hours show only that severe corner conditions did not arise during benign runs, leaving dormant failure modes untested.

Adversarial fault injection answers both traps by turning a design claim into a controlled disturbance executed inside an independent physical containment cell. To count as engineering evidence rather than an ad hoc stress test, a fault trial must declare its operating-envelope point, score telemetry against precommitted oracles that leave no room for after-the-fact negotiation, and preserve cryptographic records of the hardware and software state. The campaign then reports which points across the envelope those trials actually cover while the test harness records how each fault propagates across power rails, fieldbuses, and mechanical brakes.

Consider the warehouse mobile manipulator navigating an aisle at 1.3 m/s (chosen; see the Reader Guide) toward a rack end where a pedestrian may step out from a blind intersection. The learned proposer might abruptly stop renewing motion commands while the base continues rolling under momentum. An architectural authority diagram specifies which subsystem must take over, but that specification does not prove that the physical permission path survives the crash or that mechanical brakes engage before impact. An empirical trial that induces the fault and measures physical motion supplies evidence for the named protective response under its declared conditions. To structure that trial, the test harness must reflect how the machine partitions execution across its physical substrates. An application system-on-chip (SoC) equipped with an embedded accelerator executes the unprivileged learned proposers (the vision-language-action intent model and the chunk policy beneath it). A lockstep safety microcontroller executes the deterministic runtime enforcer. The safety microcontroller commands the base-drive motor inverters across a hard real-time fieldbus (EtherCAT, or Controller Area Network with Flexible Data-Rate, CAN FD) whose cyclic frames arrive on a strict, microsecond-scale schedule. Both processors draw power from the shared 47.7 V drive bus that powers the traction motors: the application SoC through a buck-boost converter, and the safety microcontroller through an isolated converter backed by a dedicated, held-up permission rail.

Weeks of uneventful operational logs at that speed say little about four severe hardware and timing faults that the architecture claims to contain:

  1. Proposer Stall (F1): The chunk policy, the fast proposer of Two proposers and the evidence horizon that renews motion every 50 ms, has its latest proposal successfully admitted by the permission path, renewing its chunk lease, but immediately suffers a thread deadlock, driver lockup, or thermal throttling freeze.
  2. Stale Observation (F2): A camera frame or depth tensor arrives at the permission path with an aged or corrupted hardware timestamp after silent queuing in an image signal processor (ISP) buffer or direct memory access (DMA) ring.
  3. Lost Fieldbus Frames (F3): Electromagnetic interference (EMI) from high-current inverter switching corrupts or drops a burst of EtherCAT or CAN FD frames between the safety microcontroller and the wheel actuators.
  4. Drive-Bus Droop and Feed Loss (F4a/F4b): A supply injector steps the shared 47.7 V drive bus below the 33 V lockout threshold of the converter feeding the application processor under the declared drive load (F4a), or the permission rail itself loses its primary electrical feed (F4b).

These physical failure modes F1–F4, paired with the supervisory authority transfer faults A1–A4 (formalized in table 2), organize the entire verification campaign. In theory, each threat has a dedicated countermeasure: the lease detects a hung proposer, the evidence-epoch check discards stale sensor frames, the fieldbus watchdog flags dropped communication cycles, and the isolated undervoltage supervisor manages electrical brownouts. In practice, adversarial verification injects each disturbance as the robot approaches the rack end, alongside the four authority handoff faults that the authority record hands over (Per-tick evidence tuples and the fault injection suite). It scores every stopping trial against temporal and distance bounds derived from the machine’s own stopping budget.

An injected fault converts a physical trial into valid evidence for an auditable safety case only when the experiment satisfies the five requirements of the cyber-physical test contract:

  1. A bounded claim: The test specifies the exact invariant the machine must maintain. For the mobile base at the rack end, the machine must come to a complete stop within the 1.10 m clear distance.
  2. Defined test conditions: The specification bounds every relevant physical variable, including initial velocity, payload mass, floor friction, ambient operating temperature, bus traffic saturation, and DC supply voltages.
  3. A precommitted oracle: An automated evaluation harness inspects captured telemetry against the claim without human intervention, retrospective threshold tuning, or post-hoc excuses (section 15.4 defines the oracle formally).
  4. A stated sampling and injection procedure: The test harness exercises the machine systematically across its operational envelope, deliberately triggering faults in sensors, buses, power rails, and actuators during peak mechanical and computational load.
  5. Provenance: The campaign logs complete cryptographic digests of model weights, calibration tables, sensor serial numbers, oscilloscope traces, and raw bus logs to enable matched re-execution. Software builds can be reproduced bit for bit; a physical trial can be replicated only when repeated under physical conditions that match declared tolerances.

The base’s stopping claim and the manipulator arm’s latch-force claim require separate operational envelopes, injection points, and acceptance criteria; this chapter follows the mobile base’s stopping claim. Fixing the contract starts with its second element, the declared conditions under which the claim must hold, which also locate the point beyond which nominal regulation must yield to an independent containment response.

15.2 The Operating Envelope

A mobile robot carrying a full payload down a warehouse aisle stops in a different distance when memory-bus contention delays its perception pipeline, when battery supply rails sag under acceleration, or when drive inverters heat up under sustained torque. Specifying the external operational design domain (ODD) alone therefore leaves the test conditions half-defined; an operating envelope couples those external conditions with the internal execution state of the computing, power, and actuation hardware. Every protective claim must declare both dimensions, the environmental bounds and the internal computational and electrical loads, under which it is guaranteed to hold.

For the mobile manipulator, the operating envelope declares the range of conditions over which the protective claim must hold. The campaign samples a discrete set of qualification points within that range; extending their results to untested points requires an explicit coverage argument. These points span an aisle speed of 1.3 m/s, payload masses ranging from an unloaded chassis to the full 50 kg rack capacity, and ambient operating temperatures from 5 °C to 35 °C. Surface friction must also be parameterized explicitly. In an optimistic all-weight traction screen, dry aisles require a friction coefficient of at least 0.204 to support the design braking deceleration of 2 m/s², whereas areas under the facility floor inspection regime guarantee only 0.12. The screen assumes that the driven wheels carry the entire weight; the actual base requires a qualified loaded deceleration that respects driven-wheel load share, configured braking current, and tip stability. For these inspected surfaces, the screen identifies a candidate restricted point at 1 m/s, whose friction premise must be logged in the enforcement telemetry. Verification trials on inspected floors must run against a physical test surface calibrated to this 0.12 threshold and must recalculate the oracles from the qualified loaded deceleration before testing the actual base.

Each physical configuration couples directly to the machine’s internal execution state. Trials must evaluate application-processor utilization driven to peak neural inference bursts, drive-bus voltages sweeping from a depleted 42 V up to a nominal 47.7 V, transmission jitter across EtherCAT or CAN FD buses, DRAM crossbar contention, and the thermal state of the safety microcontroller. The verification campaign also challenges transient edge conditions, chief among them a human takeover while the base is moving. Clearance-derived fallback deadlines screens a candidate in-loop takeover, with its 605 ms conservative response-and-exchange allowance, at the 0.75 m/s slow-down; the same moving handover is infeasible at the 1.3 m/s aisle speed and must be rejected. The slow-down remains a qualification candidate, conditional on operator response, admission delay, current clearance, and a feasible stopping continuation. The operative design stops the base before handing over. Both speeds therefore belong in the campaign, one to challenge a candidate moving transition and one to verify its rejection. Whenever an authority transition cannot preserve the verified stopping envelope, the enforcer must execute a local stop.

↩︎ The rule that an authority transition unable to preserve the stopping envelope ends in a local stop is established in Clearance-derived fallback deadlines.

Definition 15.1: Operating envelope

Operating envelope is the declared set of external conditions and internal execution states, each point stated with its units and tolerances, over which a machine’s protective claim must hold and at which every trial of that claim is run.

  1. Significance: The envelope governs which terms of the inequality \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) are stressed during an empirical trial. For the running machine, an aisle speed of 1.3 m/s falls below the optimistic 1.44 m/s ceiling on a dry floor, yet exceeds the 1.23 m/s ceiling on an inspected surface. The identical velocity therefore passes the all-weight screen on one surface and fails it on another; passing still requires loaded qualification before admission.
  2. Distinction: The operational design domain describes the external environment encountered by the planning policy. The operating envelope expands this definition to incorporate internal machine states, including processor utilization, drive-bus voltages between 42 V and 47.7 V, and communication bus saturation. The same physical aisle yields different safety margins under memory contention or electrical depletion.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent mistake is treating an operating envelope as a simple Cartesian product of decoupled intervals, assuming that testing each parameter independently at its minimum and maximum guarantees coverage. In cyber-physical systems, multi-dimensional boundary couplings are the rule rather than the exception: two parameters operating well within their nominal isolated bounds can combine to trigger an unrecoverable failure. Robust verification demands testing directly at these coupled, high-stress corners.

An operating envelope must be distinguished from the wider survival envelope that encloses it. Within the operating envelope, the learned policy and low-level tracking controllers regulate nominal behavior. When an external disturbance, sensor dropout, or compute stall pushes the machine across that boundary, nominal control yields to safety mechanisms that bring the robot to a bounded, safe stop before structural limits are violated. For the mobile manipulator, the survival envelope must be characterized separately for each fallback response and machine state. The standard resident stop executes controlled, powered deceleration before setting electromechanical holding brakes at zero velocity. In contrast, torque inhibit prevents commanded torque generation without necessarily electrically isolating the drive; it does not follow the controlled stopping profile or inherit its stopping-distance budget. If the remaining containment mechanisms cannot absorb the motion, structural damage may result when kinetic energy (\(\KineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \Mass \ImpactSpeed^2\)) exceeds what mechanical compliance and chassis end-stop dampening can absorb before hard impact. For the base in a test cell, the survival envelope is therefore the set of states from which the qualified combination of torque inhibit, passive resistance, holding mechanisms, and compliant end-stops can contain the remaining motion and energy. That boundary, not the clear distance of the stopping claim, is what the test cell’s own protection watches.

↩︎ The fallback rungs, from the controlled resident stop to hardware torque inhibit, are established in The four rungs of the fallback ladder.

Establishing envelope boundaries requires deriving physical constraints from first principles rather than asserting arbitrary software timeouts. In rack-end operations, this boundary is governed by the stopping budget. Total stopping distance scales linearly with pre-braking latency and quadratically with vehicle speed (equation 2.2). In the conditional design budget of The warehouse mobile manipulator's stopping budget, a pre-brake subtotal of 133.6 ms measured from sensor capture, combined with the jerk-limited resident stop and the tracking inset, yields a partial-budget speed ceiling of 1.44 m/s on a dry floor. That ceiling holds only before the dispatch-to-admission interval is charged, and The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval allocated that interval without yet measuring it. Running at the candidate aisle velocity of 1.3 m/s consumes 969.0 mm of the available 1.10 m clearance. The unallocated spare of 131.0 mm, which the vehicle crosses in 100.8 ms, must still absorb that interval. Its allocation of 63.3 ms costs 82.3 mm of travel at this speed and leaves 48.7 mm, or 37.5 ms of waiting, the same remainder that Clearance-derived fallback deadlines found for a human takeover at aisle speed. The full 100.8 ms is therefore a ceiling, the longest dispatch-to-admission interval the spare could pay for. When later examples in this chapter quote the full spare, they quote an upper bound on the unbudgeted delay the machine can tolerate; once the allocation is charged, only the remainder is left to spend.

↩︎ The jerk-limited resident stop and its stopping distance are established in The C^2 stopping suffix and jerk-limited braking.

Two conditional oracle bounds follow from that budget. Before any qualification trial, the harness must show that the bounds cover the whole execution path from the reference event to rest, because an interval left unallocated is an open qualification gap rather than a margin.

Of the 82 ms onset deadline, the lease takes 60 ms.

  1. Powered Deceleration Onset Deadline: Powered deceleration must begin no later than 82 ms after the last valid renewal. This deadline traverses the full permission and actuation chain: the 60 ms lease duration, a single 1 ms tick of the safety permission loop, one 1 ms communication bus cycle, and the 20 ms drive latency from delivered stop command to physical deceleration onset. (For the power-rail fault scenario F4, the onset deadline is referenced instead to the assertion of the hardware undervoltage flag.)
  2. Stopping Distance Bound: The vehicle, tracked by external optical instruments independent of onboard odometry, must come to rest within the distance allocated from the reference event that triggers the fault. Three reference events are possible, and figure 1 places them on one time axis. A capture-referenced trial measures clearance and displacement from the exposure midpoint of a qualified sensor capture; the 969.0 mm subtotal starts there but omits the dispatch-to-admission interval, so it is incomplete until that interval is added. An appearance-referenced trial starts earlier, at the optical appearance of an unheralded hazard. It must timestamp that appearance and the first usable capture independently, and add the travel between them, as well as the dispatch-to-admission travel, to obtain a complete bound against the clearance measured at appearance. A renewal-referenced trial applies when a stalled proposer produces no new target at all; it scores both stopping distance and onset latency from the last valid lease renewal. No trial may claim a pass against an incomplete budget subtotal.

↩︎ The lease path, from the last admission through the permission tick and bus cycle to brake onset, is established in Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget.

Measured from that event, the two F1 verification predicates are (equation 1): \[\HazardOnset \le \ChunkLease + \PermissionLoopPeriod + \CommandTransportTime + \ActuatorResponseTime, \qquad \StallTravelBound(\SpeedAtDetection) = \SpeedAtDetection\,(\ChunkLease + \PermissionLoopPeriod + \CommandTransportTime + \ActuatorResponseTime) + \ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection) + \TrackingErrorBound \tag{1}\] Here \(\HazardOnset\) is the measured interval from the last valid renewal to the physical onset of powered deceleration, \(\StallTravelBound(\SpeedAtDetection)\) bounds the measured travel from that renewal to rest, \(\ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)\) is the jerk-limited resident stop profile from equation 11.17, and \(\TrackingErrorBound\) is the tracking inset. The design budget’s pre-brake subtotal \(\PrebrakeDelay\) begins earlier, at raw sensor capture, and its sum also carries the localization bound and the protective margin. Notebook 15.1 evaluates both sums at the rack end.

Napkin Math 15.1: The reference-event gap
Problem: A proposer stall at the aisle speed is scored from the last valid renewal. What onset and travel bounds follow from the lease path and the resident stop, and how far below the capture-referenced subtotal of the design budget do they sit?

Variables:

  • \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 1.3 m/s (Chosen) and \(\ClearDistance =\) 1.10 m (Illustrative; The running machine's card).
  • \(\ChunkLease =\) 60 ms and \(\PermissionLoopPeriod =\) 1 ms (Chosen); \(\CommandTransportTime =\) 1 ms and \(\ActuatorResponseTime =\) 20 ms (Illustrative).
  • \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration =\) 2 m/s² (Illustrative; stands in for a measured loaded deceleration) and \(\JerkLimit =\) 7.11 m/s³ (Derived; the reference stop polynomial’s peak jerk).
  • \(\TrackingErrorBound =\) 40 mm and \(\LocalizationBound =\) 50 mm (Illustrative); \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin =\) 100 mm (Chosen); evidence age \(\ObservationAge =\) 51.6 ms (Derived; The latency waterfall).

Math:

Onset bound. \[t_{\text{onset}} \le T_{\text{lease}} + T_{\text{tick}} + T_{\text{bus}} + T_{\text{act}} = 60 + 1 + 1 + 20 = 82\ \text{ms}\]

Resident stop (equation 11.17). \[d_{\text{suffix}}(v) = \frac{v^2}{2a_{\text{brake}}} + \frac{v\,a_{\text{brake}}}{2j_{\max}} = \frac{1.3^2}{2 \times 2} + \frac{1.3 \times 2}{2 \times 7.11} = 0.4225 + 0.1828 = 0.6053\ \text{m}\]

F1 oracle from the last valid renewal (equation 1). \[d_{\text{F1}}(v) = 1.3 \times 82 + 605.3 + 40 = 106.6 + 605.3 + 40 = 751.9\ \text{mm}\]

Capture-referenced subtotal, which leaves 131.0 mm of the clear distance. \[v\,\tau_{\text{delay}} + d_{\text{suffix}} + \delta_{\text{loc}} + \delta_{\text{margin}} + \epsilon_{\text{track}} = 1.3 \times 133.6 + 605.3 + 50 + 100 + 40 = 969.0\ \text{mm}\]

The gap between them. \[969.0 - 751.9 = 1.3 \times 51.6 + 50 + 100 = 67.1 + 150 = 217.1\ \text{mm}\]

Sensitivity. In the optimistic all-weight screen at the restricted point on the inspected floor, friction caps the deceleration at 1.18 m/s²: \[d_{\text{F1}}(1) = 1 \times 82 + \left(\frac{1^2}{2 \times 1.177} + \frac{1 \times 1.177}{2 \times 7.11}\right)\text{m} + 40 = 82 + 507.6 + 40 = 629.6\ \text{mm}\] A speed reduction of 23.1 percent shortens the oracle distance by only 16.3 percent, because the diminished traction cancels out the majority of the kinematic savings.

Result: F1 passes only if powered deceleration begins within 82 ms and the base travels at most 751.9 mm from the last valid renewal, 217.1 mm less than the capture-referenced subtotal.

Systems insight: Both formulations share the identical deceleration trajectory \(\ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)\); they diverge entirely on their reference event. The design subtotal incorporates 67.1 mm of evidence-age travel alongside the localization bound and protective margin that protect a detected target. However, a stalled proposer produces no target. If the verification harness evaluated an F1 proposer stall against this generous capture-referenced subtotal, the distance predicate would tolerate a braking onset roughly 167 ms late, more than double the entire onset deadline. Verifying the invariant \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) remains sound only when travel is measured strictly from the physical or temporal event that the fault disrupts.

The partial budget beneath both bounds reproduces the arithmetic of the design record, serving as a conditional oracle specification rather than empirical timing closure. A physical trial must directly instrument and log successful admission timestamps, continuing evidence age, and physical deceleration onset. Nominal proposer loop cadences cannot guarantee that lease renewals succeed before the deadline expires. Because a safety lease begins strictly upon successful admission, the renewal verification campaign must qualify the maximum inter-admission interval demanded by the lease rule (equation 4.4) under realistic compute contention, bus saturation, and rejected proposals, rather than measuring only unvetted proposer dispatch timestamps.

Both oracle bounds also assume that the safety permission pipeline, required perception channels, and powered traction drives survive the injected fault. Capacitive hold-up for logic rails and brake coils cannot sustain the traction drives during heavy braking. The power-loss fallback (F4b) must therefore maintain and monitor a verified auxiliary power reserve and dynamic braking dissipation path throughout deceleration. If an injected fault disables these electrical reserves, the system drops into an unpowered fallback mode whose stopping envelope must be characterized independently. Neither oracle bound relies on corrective action from either learned proposer. When the chunk policy stalls, it cannot delay braking onset: the hardware safety monitor issues the stop when the lease timer expires, with physical onset following the allocated tick, bus, and actuator path, cleanly isolating safety enforcement from proposer latency. The F1 qualification test thus benchmarks the deterministic safety permission path rather than the learned policy. Finally, this stopping budget assumes a static obstacle located at the clearance boundary. Static verification trials do not validate protection against dynamic agents moving toward the machine; site crossing restrictions and occluded-motion tracking must cover that case. Declaring these qualification points alongside both formal bounds provides the complete specification required to execute an empirical verification trial.

↩︎ Tracking a moving body through occlusion, which this static-obstacle budget does not cover, is established in Forward State Propagation and Occlusion Decay.

15.3 One F1 Trial from Injection to Record

The campaign’s first trial is F1, a proposer failure during transit, planned at the candidate restricted envelope point of 1 m/s with the full declared payload on a test floor qualified to the inspected friction. The numerical oracle remains a screening specification until loaded braking qualification supplies the actual deceleration bound. The safety claim under test is conditional. Once trajectory proposal renewals cease, the low-level permission path and motor drives must autonomously execute the resident powered stop within their allocated stopping envelope. The test deliberately halts or freezes the chunk policy immediately following its last valid lease renewal while leaving its underlying timer heartbeat active. This fault pattern cleanly separates true algorithmic progress in the proposer from the superficial liveness of an unblocked timer interrupt.

Verifying this stopping claim in its entirety requires a full physical rig: the production mobile base, motor drives, representative payload mass, and a qualified test surface. A processor-in-the-loop (PIL) bench, which runs the enforcer’s compiled binary on its target microcontroller against a simulated plant, can validate lease expiration but not the plant’s response, and a pure software simulation approximates both the target’s execution timing and the contact mechanics. Neither bench environment alone provides empirical ground truth for physical stopping distance. Because the machine operates at the edge of its operational envelope during fault injection, an external physical containment fixture must guard the testing volume independently of whatever safety action the machine itself takes.

The temporal reference event for F1 is the last successful admission check that renewed the trajectory lease, not the upstream planner dispatch or the subsequent fault injection trigger. A complete diagnostic trace captures both the final renewal and the injection event across isolated hardware channels that independently record lease expiration, the permission path’s stop command, the physical onset of powered deceleration, and external tracking of base position through complete standstill. Calibrated motor-current or torque measurements alongside wheel encoders identify drive effort and wheel response; external base-motion tracking is still needed to establish chassis deceleration under possible wheel slip. Timing uncertainty belongs in the onset margin. An auxiliary contact switch on the mechanical brake verifies subsequent holding-brake engagement. Relying on the mechanical brake switch alone would mischaracterize stopping latency, as it cannot record the initial phase of regenerative or active electric braking.

Instrumentation must capture the machine’s state trajectory, fault detector identity, refusal timestamp, and recovery time to a safe state, and it must be electrically and logically decoupled from the robot. Examples are external optical ground-truth cameras and optical shaft encoders routed to isolated digitizers. An onboard logging daemon cannot serve, because the fault under evaluation, whether a panicked kernel or a saturated interconnect bus, delays or drops the very log it would write.2 Independent external measurement helps expose false passes, where an anomalous run appears successful for reasons outside the formal safety claim. For example, injecting a dropped-frame fault might unintentionally trigger an unmonitored thermal breaker or an auxiliary ground loop that cuts drive power. If that happens, the spring-applied mechanical brake halts the base well within the distance budget, even while the software safety enforcer remains deadlocked in a priority inversion. A naive verification oracle that checks only final stopping distance would mark that trial as a success. A rigorous test harness must verify that the exact causal enforcer path specified in the safety claim commanded and governed the deceleration.

2 Probe Effect: Gait (1986) identified the probe effect in concurrent software systems, where the overhead of monitoring instrumentation alters execution timing, inadvertently masking or introducing race conditions. On-chip hardware trace units, such as Arm’s CoreSight Embedded Trace Macrocell, stream cycle-accurate instruction flows over dedicated trace pins, while external protocol analyzers monitor bus transactions without altering bus arbitration. A verification trial must time the enforcer’s reaction using non-intrusive hardware instruments rather than software logging daemons executing on the processor under test.

Gait, Jason. 1986. “A Probe Effect in Concurrent Programs.” Software: Practice and Experience 16 (3): 225–33. https://doi.org/10.1002/spe.4380160304.

Superficial liveness signals are a second source of false passes. Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts required that a watchdog be refreshed only when a safety-critical cycle completes, never from an independent periodic interrupt. That section grounded the rule in the Toyota unintended-acceleration litigation, where expert testimony argued that a timer interrupt could keep a hardware watchdog satisfied after the control tasks had stopped (Barr 2013). A design rule on paper does not show that the built machine obeys it, and F1 is the test of whether it does. The harness halts the chunk policy’s task or freezes its command output while keeping the periodic interrupt handler alive (perspective 15.1), and it measures the actuator response and watchdog state with external instruments. A host daemon can keep reporting a valid process heartbeat after the policy inference thread has frozen, so trial F1 passes only when physical deceleration onset is recorded at the motor drives, never on the strength of a software heartbeat logged in memory. The Toyota record adds a second question that the design rule alone does not raise, whether the supervising monitor shares an input with the processor it supervises (war story 15.1).

Systems Perspective 15.1: The watchdog liveness invariant
A watchdog serviced by a periodic timer interrupt proves only that the timer interrupt service routine executed, not that safety-critical application tasks met their deadlines, computed valid outputs, or avoided memory corruption. A qualified fault-injection campaign explicitly decouples application progress from interrupt liveness, verifying that the system initiates physical shutdown under task termination, thread starvation, and frozen control registers.

War Story 15.1: Testing a task-blind watchdog hypothesis
Context: In Bookout v. Toyota (Oklahoma County, 2013), a jury heard expert testimony on the electronic throttle control system (ETCS-i) of a 2005 Camry. The plaintiffs’ software expert argued that safety-critical control tasks could fail silently while supervisory mechanisms remained blind to the failure (Barr 2013). Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts drew its watchdog rule from this testimony; the same record also bears on a second claim, the independence of the monitor that backs the watchdog.

Mechanism: The engine control module serviced its hardware watchdog from a recurring timer-tick interrupt rather than requiring verified progress from active control tasks. The expert testified that if the task managing throttle angle and critical fail-safes terminated due to memory corruption or a stack overflow, the throttle command could freeze at its last valid state while the timer interrupt continued to pacify the watchdog (Barr 2013). The architecture included a secondary monitor processor, but testimony highlighted that this monitor could not inspect task scheduling; it could detect a dead throttle task only indirectly, through changes in brake-pedal state. A single analog-to-digital converter also digitized accelerator-pedal position for both processors, placing a common-cause failure point across the supervision boundary (Dunn 2013). Recovery could therefore require the counterintuitive act of lifting a foot off the brake during the event.

Impact: On October 24, 2013, the jury awarded $3 million in compensatory damages and found that Toyota had acted with reckless disregard; the parties settled before the jury could consider punitive damages (Safety Research & Strategies 2013). The verdict did not establish an uncontested technical cause. NASA’s engineering review for the federal investigation found no evidence that an electronic malfunction caused large unintended accelerations (NASA Engineering and Safety Center 2011). It did document electronic faults, among them a tin whisker that formed a 248 Ω short within an accelerator-pedal position sensor from a 2003 Camry. Vehicle tests confirmed that while this fault altered how the throttle tracked the pedal, the throttle still returned to idle whenever the pedal was released (Leidecker et al. 2011). The investigation attributed the explainable incidents to floor-mat entrapment, sticking pedals, and pedal misapplication. The task-blind mechanism therefore remains an attributed hypothesis, which is exactly the kind of claim only injection can settle.

Response: For the design under test, fault injection must establish which application progress checks reach the watchdog, verify whether the independent monitor shares any input pathway with the processor it supervises (section 15.6.2), and confirm which protective inputs can reliably command a physically suitable stop. The historical controversy highlights the danger, but only direct injection testing establishes these properties for the warehouse machine.

Systems lesson: A periodic watchdog heartbeat confirms only that an interrupt handler continues to cycle, not that application tasks are functioning. A monitor is independent only if no input, supply, or clock it depends on can fail together with the processor it supervises. Verification must therefore terminate the supervised task and inject faults at those shared points, measuring the actuator response with external, isolated instruments.

Leidecker, Henning, Lyudmyla Panashchenko, and Jay Brusse. 2011. Electrical Failure of an Accelerator Pedal Position Sensor Caused by a Tin Whisker and Discussion of Investigative Techniques Used for Whisker Detection. 5th International Tin Whisker Symposium.
NASA Engineering and Safety Center. 2011. Technical Support to the NHTSA on the Reported Toyota Motor Corporation (TMC) Unintended Acceleration Investigation. National Aeronautics; Space Administration.
Safety Research & Strategies. 2013. Toyota Unintended Acceleration and the Big Bowl of “Spaghetti” Code.
Dunn, Michael. 2013. Toyota’s Killer Firmware: Bad Design and Its Consequences. EDN.
Barr, Michael. 2013. Bookout v. Toyota Motor Corp.: 2005 Camry L4 Software Analysis.

Safety intervention mechanisms must be evaluated under the exact operational stress that makes intervention necessary. A safety enforcer that takes over cleanly on an idle development bench offers little assurance for a production vehicle. In an operational emergency, the policy model operates at maximum inference throughput, memory buses are saturated with high-resolution sensor streams, DMA controllers move camera frame buffers, and the mobile base carries its loaded rack at aisle speed. The verification framework therefore injects faults under declared worst-case thermal and electrical loads, measuring both lease-expiration revocation latency and the elapsed time between diagnostic fault assertion and powered deceleration onset at the motor drives. If the intervention path shares a system bus or memory arbiter with the neural accelerator, safety takeover can stall behind an unpreemptible burst transfer. A safety case that assumes near-instantaneous intervention without a contention bound can credit a response the deployed hardware cannot deliver.

↩︎ How a small urgent read waits behind a large burst transfer on a shared memory interconnect is established in Contention for Shared Resources.

3 Crowbar: The name comes from the blunt industrial act of dropping a metal bar across power supply terminals. A crowbar circuit intentionally shorts a supply rail when an overvoltage occurs, forcing an upstream fuse or breaker to trip before hazardous energy reaches sensitive loads. Within a physical fault-injection test cell, this containment concept protects physical safety at the actuator stage: independent ground-truth sensors detecting an envelope violation trigger a hardware disconnect that severs actuator drive power over a dedicated electrical pathway that the vehicle under test can neither power nor override. Physical containment therefore never depends on the correctness of the very enforcer undergoing evaluation.

A verification campaign executes every injection trial under a deterministic, automated protocol, ensuring that verdicts derive strictly from repeatable physical measurements rather than post hoc interpretation. The test orchestrator first audits all operating preconditions (ambient temperature, supply rail voltages, baseline wheel speed) against declared engineering tolerances; any trial starting out of bounds aborts with a verdict of INVALID, which charges the failure to the fixture rather than the machine, before fault injection begins. Once preconditions hold, the orchestrator synchronizes the external plant fixture, logic analyzer, and independent ground-truth digitizers to a common reference clock, while recording the target clocks without disciplining away their injected drift. After allowing the mobile base to settle at its envelope operating point, the orchestrator asserts the fault trigger across a dedicated hardware line, registering the fault injection timestamp on the unified timeline. The isolated logic analyzer captures both this injection mark and the formal reference event (the final valid lease renewal in trial F1), so fault detection latency, drive takeover duration, and total stopping time are each scored from their true temporal origins. Concurrently, external truth sensors tied to a fail-safe crowbar relay circuit3 stand ready to initiate the test cell’s qualified containment response within a measured response bound if the machine approaches its survival-envelope limit. A precommitted evaluation oracle then scores the synchronized trace against the pass/fail criteria defined in section 15.4. Runs with corrupted or dropped external measurement frames are flagged transparently as UNOBSERVABLE, the verdict for a trial whose scoring telemetry was lost (section 15.4.1 sets out all four verdicts). Finally, the test orchestrator cryptographically signs the complete trial record header and seals it into the safety evidence bundle via a cryptographic digest.

↪ Protocol: The fault-injection protocol, stage by stage with its error codes, is given in Fault-injection protocol for a reader building the test orchestrator.

15.4 Fault Oracles and Acceptance

A valid verification trace for the F1 safety claim must establish both timely deceleration onset and bounded stopping distance; observing that the robot base eventually stopped establishes neither guarantee. Without an oracle fixed in advance, post-hoc inspection takes over: engineers eyeball time-series plots after the run, seizing on gross symptoms such as whether Joule heating melted a motor phase wire or a controller threw an unhandled exception. The trial degenerates into an anecdote.

Definition 15.2: Test oracle

Test oracle is the predicate, fixed in full before a trial runs, that compares each independently witnessed quantity of the trace with its bound from a declared reference event and returns the trial’s verdict.

  1. Significance: For F1, the oracle comprises two predicates evaluated from the last valid renewal: powered deceleration onset within 82 ms and total travel within 751.9 mm at aisle speed. Each predicate evaluates to a signed margin in its native physical unit. Retaining signed margins rather than binary passes enables near misses to inform subsequent trials and guides adversarial search algorithms toward razor-thin safety margins.
  2. Distinction: A pass criterion extracted from telemetry after a run is chosen with the outcome already visible; an oracle is mathematically precommitted. The oracle also differs from the design budget from which it is derived: the 969.0 mm design subtotal starts at sensor capture and protects a planned target trajectory, whereas a stalled proposer produces no planned trajectory to protect.
  3. Common pitfall: A persistent misconception in physical verification is that a robot base stopping short of a rack signifies a passed trial. In reality, stopping within the 1.10 m clearance distance can easily conceal a safety controller that missed its deceleration deadline, or a stop triggered by an unmodeled side effect, such as a tripped thermal breaker that cut drive-bus power, rather than the deterministic safety path named in the claim.

15.4.1 Four verdicts and the invalid-test rule

If traction control derates motor torque over an uneven floor seam and slows the mobile base before fault injection, the robot encounters the fault below its declared operating speed. Conversely, if a hardware frame injector overflows its queue and drops an adversarial CAN message before it reaches the transceiver, the robot never encounters the fault at all. Scoring either aborted run as a pass manufactures artificial confidence, while scoring it as a system failure blames the vehicle for a defective test harness. Rigorous verification therefore enforces an invalid-test rule that separates testbed anomalies from the machine’s own performance.

Under that rule, the oracle returns one of four explicit verdicts. A PASS indicates that every precommitted timing and physical predicate held under verified preconditions. A FAIL indicates that the system violated one or more measured predicates. An INVALID verdict indicates that the fixture failed to establish declared test preconditions or the fault injection itself miscarried. An UNOBSERVABLE verdict indicates that ground-truth telemetry required to score the trial was corrupted or lost, while the partial trace is retained for diagnosis. Under an INVALID or UNOBSERVABLE verdict, any unmeasured quantity is logged as unavailable rather than set to zero. Neither verdict may ever be converted into a PASS, and neither is ever omitted from aggregate coverage reports. An UNOBSERVABLE trial supplies no verdict on the unmeasured claim; its missing evidence requires investigating both the witness system and the injected fault, rather than assuming the harness alone failed. Silently discarding runs with dropped telemetry or treating unobservable outcomes as passes introduces survivorship bias. If an inductive kick from an emergency brake solenoid emits an electromagnetic interference burst that crashes the data acquisition (DAQ) logger, discarding that run conceals the exact cyber-physical failure under test.

Precommitment separates empirical evidence from after-the-fact excuses. In physical fault injection, cyber-physical interactions are noisy and counterintuitive, so criteria negotiated after inspecting the trace inevitably drift to accommodate anomalous behavior. An unexpected overrun is easily dismissed as floor variation, or a deceleration onset that exceeds the 82 ms bound is excused because the vehicle came to rest before hitting the rack.

A precommitted record prevents that drift by locking down every physical predicate, measurement unit, normalization scale, response-time bound, and invalidation trigger before the first fault is triggered. The evaluation engine computes and logs a signed margin for each predicate, issuing a PASS verdict only when the declared preconditions and injection are verified, all required predicates are observable, and every timing bound and physical margin remains non-negative. If stopping distance exceeds its limit by five millimeters or powered braking begins \(2\text{ ms}\) past its deadline, the record pinpoints the exact predicate that failed so engineers can trace whether the cause was degraded tire-floor friction, mechanical brake lag, motor thermal derating, or software diagnostic latency. Precommitment enforces the fourth law, permission must be earned (principle 4), at the test boundary. Production deployment grants operational authority on the basis of these records, so any verdict scored under rules relaxed after the fact would grant authority on the accident of an outcome rather than the guarantee of a verified machine.

15.4.2 The F1 record and its missing coverage

A stopping trace separated from its precondition checks or oracle digest cannot prove which threshold it was scored against or whether the floor and payload matched the claim. A fault record prevents that separation by cryptographically binding a finalized metadata header to the independently retained sensorimotor trace. Identity fields in the header specify the trial identifier and safety claim, the physical envelope point, the fault injection parameters (mode, magnitude, and target subsystem), and a cryptographic digest of the precommitted oracle. Outcome fields record the measured detection and takeover latencies, the signed physical margins, the four-state verdict, a digest of the complete evidence bundle, and an asymmetric digital signature over the entire header, while the high-rate telemetry trace is archived inside the bundle.

The planned F1 record at the candidate restricted envelope point, 1 m/s on an inspected floor with a 50 kg payload, fills these fields (table 1). As with every proposer stall, the stopping distance oracle is anchored to the timestamp of the last valid lease renewal. Under the optimistic all-weight screen on a floor with friction coefficient \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient =\) 0.12, equation 1 yields a stopping bound of 629.6 mm (corresponding to the sensitivity boundary in notebook 15.1). This distance is strictly shorter than the 831.2 mm capture-referenced budget subtotal at that speed. The capture-referenced subtotal accounts for upstream sensor evidence age and fixed geometric margins while omitting dispatch-to-admission communication delay; consequently, it cannot be substituted for the renewal-referenced oracle of F1 or an end-to-end appearance-referenced bound. Of the three bounds, only the renewal-referenced one contains the complete timing path; its braking premise still awaits loaded qualification. The capture-referenced subtotal has a value but omits the dispatch-to-admission interval. The appearance-referenced bound has no value at all, because it still lacks a witnessed appearance-to-capture interval as well as a measured dispatch-to-admission interval (figure 1).

In the planned record (table 1), the outcome field contains no numerical value and is marked unavailable. Because no physical trials have been executed in this book, the field specifies the physical measurement that will eventually populate it; until empirical data are recorded and verified by independent witnesses, the record remains uncommitted and carries no verdict. The trial count in that field is a design choice of its own. The number of passing trials needed to bound the miss probability follows the zero-failure count of Sample size requirements per empirical claim, which section 15.10 applies to an injected fault population.

↪ Byte layout: The record header, field by field and including how an unavailable value is encoded, is given in Fault record for a reader building the record.

The record schema maintains strict forensic separation among verdicts on a per-field basis. If an upstream breaker trips and prevents an injection, the harness marks the run INVALID and records the detection latency, takeover delay, and stopping margin as unavailable rather than coercing them to zero. Because the evidence bundle attaches an explicit validity flag and missing-data reason code to each measurement, the scoring oracle can inspect those flags before running any statistical or arithmetic evaluation, keeping unmeasured fields from corrupting pass-rate calculations, latency percentiles, or margin statistics.

A single aggregate percentage conceals where the verification campaign never ventured. A system that survives 98 percent of five thousand fault injection trials demonstrates little if every trial ran at nominal room temperature under negligible memory bus contention. Comprehensive verification requires reporting coverage across orthogonal dimensions: functional safety claims, physical envelope extremes, physical and logical injection sites, timing distribution tails, hardware build revisions, and concurrent multi-fault scenarios. For the warehouse mobile manipulator, physical envelope coverage verifies whether faults were injected under both empty and fully loaded rack states, at nominal aisle speed on dry concrete, at restricted speed on inspected surfaces, and under depleted DC drive-bus voltages. Timing tail coverage isolates injections scheduled to coincide with p99 operating system scheduling jitter in the permission loop or saturated DMA bus traffic. System verification cannot be declared complete when an aggregate coverage score exceeds a target percentage; rather, the minimum coverage across every individual dimension must satisfy the safety case requirements.4

4 Scope of assurance standards: ISO 26262-5 addresses road-vehicle electrical and electronic hardware. Its single-point and latent fault metrics concern the architecture and its allocated safety goals; they are not the failure-rate-weighted aggregate coverage against which notebook 15.3 later sizes an injection campaign. Fault injection is one way to test diagnostic assumptions, alongside analysis and other evidence. ANSI/UL 4600 frames an autonomous-vehicle safety case from multiple kinds of evidence. Neither standard makes a fixed number of hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) trials sufficient for release.

Repeated trials provide statistical evidence only when underlying physical or digital states vary. Executing a deterministic fault script five hundred times on a digital bench with identical initial register states, fixed clock dividers, and synchronized inputs traverses the exact same execution path on every iteration; it contributes no more statistical information than a single run. Conversely, on a physical test aisle, repetition samples real-world stochasticity: micro-variations in wheel-floor contact friction, asynchronous hardware interrupt arrivals, bus arbitration jitter, and phase drift between the camera exposure clock and the policy inference cycle. In that setting, repeated trials characterize the variance and map the dangerous tails of the end-to-end response-time distribution.

Checkpoint 15.1: Oracles, verdicts, and reference events

An F1 result carries meaning only through the oracle, reference event, and verdict rule fixed before the run.

Scoring a trial

Estimating a margin

15.5 Extending the Trial to the Envelope

The F1 record fixes one trial at one envelope point, and even a clean pass there says nothing about the points around it. The operating-envelope definition already warned that the danger lies at coupled corners, where two parameters that are each within their declared ranges combine to break the claim. Extending the trial therefore means choosing which further points to test, and that choice follows from how the machine’s physics couples its variables.

15.5.1 Coupled envelope corners versus one-variable sweeps

Sweeping travel speed alone on dry concrete and floor friction alone at a crawl passes both checks while missing the corner where aisle speed meets an inspected floor. Because physical mechanics, compute pipeline latency, and electrical power distribution interact at run time, independent minimum and maximum sweeps for individual variables leave the dangerous corners of the operating envelope untested:

The 1.3 m/s aisle speed clears the dry-floor ceiling but not the inspected floor’s.

  • Surface friction and travel speed: The 1.3 m/s aisle speed and an inspected floor friction of 0.12 each fall well within their declared individual operating ranges. In combination, however, they fail even the optimistic all-weight traction screen. On low-friction surfaces, the tire-floor interface, not mechanical braking capacity, sets an upper limit on deceleration; in the all-weight screen it falls to 1.18 m/s². Consequently, the resident stopping maneuver elongates: the required stopping distance at 1.3 m/s expands to 1.19 m, exceeding the available 1.10 m clearance. The optimistic screening ceiling on that floor drops to 1.23 m/s, and the actual speed limit must use the qualified loaded lower bound rather than assuming this ceiling is attainable.
  • Contact nonlinearities and dynamic load transfer: Real warehouse floors rarely have a constant friction coefficient \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\). Instead, surface friction varies dynamically based on local contamination (such as concrete dust, ambient moisture, or oil films), wheel tread heating, and transient wheel loading. On the running machine only the two driven hub wheels brake against the floor, so the traction ceiling is \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \Gravity \DrivenWheelLoadFraction\), where \(\DrivenWheelLoadFraction\) is the share of the weight those wheels carry (derived in the free-body diagram of The Physical Body, figure). During hard braking, the inertial load \(\Mass\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) acting at the center of gravity pitches the body forward and can move normal force off the driven wheels onto the front caster, lowering \(\DrivenWheelLoadFraction\) exactly when traction is needed. If the drives demand braking torque sized for the static load share, the driven wheels slip and the net braking force collapses. Under a constant-friction approximation, the braking distance then grows as \(\SpeedAtDetection^2 / (2\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \Gravity \DrivenWheelLoadFraction)\), inversely with the friction actually delivered at the driven wheels and quadratically with speed.
  • Computational and electrical coupling: Memory and power subsystems couple directly into safety margins. A compute-heavy forward pass of the vision-language-action intent model executing on the application SoC can saturate unified memory bandwidth. This contention delays camera-buffer processing and postpones the compact evidence handoff to the safety microcontroller, inflating the observation age and, with it, the pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\). Because stopping distance scales linearly with total reaction delay (equation 2.2), a 30 ms memory stall consumes 39 mm of physical clearance at aisle speed. On the electrical bus, the dominant transient coupling originates from the motor drive actuators rather than the neural compute accelerator. At its maximum 60 W draw, the SoC pulls approximately 1.6 A from a depleted battery bus, only 2.2 percent of the overall 70 A transient, whereas the two drive motors demand up to 50 A during peak torque transients. Severe acceleration can sag the common DC bus across battery internal resistance and wiring harness impedance, while bursty inference activity primarily stresses the SoC’s local point-of-load regulators, a localized transient that the F4 campaign must isolate and measure separately.
  • Payload mass and kinematic posture: Arm posture and payload distribution couple directly to stopping performance through vehicle stability and load transfer. Under braking, the base tips about its front caster whenever deceleration exceeds \(\Gravity\,\LeadingSupportDistance/\GravityCenterHeight\), where \(\GravityCenterHeight\) is the height of the center of gravity and \(\LeadingSupportDistance\) its distance behind that caster. This is the tip-over ceiling computed in Electrical time constants, back-EMF, and current clamping, which the credible deceleration respects only while \(\GravityCenterHeight/\LeadingSupportDistance\) stays below the ratio computed there. Raising the arm above a storage rack raises \(\GravityCenterHeight\), and reaching forward shortens \(\LeadingSupportDistance\), so posture moves the machine toward that ceiling. The tip-over threshold can bind before tire traction saturates, and ISO 3691-4 mandates physical stability verification for driverless industrial trucks (International Organization for Standardization 2023). Unloaded trials support only their tested configurations; they cannot establish the fully loaded deceleration or the complete operating envelope. The braking-limit check of Kinematic stopping distance and pre-brake delay left both lengths unmeasured, so the envelope cannot yet certify that the demanded deceleration stays below the tipping threshold; a tilt-table test or multi-axis corner-scale measurement of the fully loaded machine must supply them.
  • Thermal throttling and fallback execution: Sustained thermal stress can induce dynamic processor throttling, delaying action proposal generation and degrading mission throughput. In an architecture protected by an independent, verified resident stop, a missed watchdog renewal safely triggers a bounded, fail-safe stopping trajectory rather than indefinitely expanding stopping delay. Nevertheless, multi-axis compound verification must ensure that simultaneous bus contention, sensor packet drops, or voltage droops do not compromise that fallback routine itself.
International Organization for Standardization. 2023. ISO 3691-4:2023: Industrial Trucks, Safety Requirements and Verification, Part 4: Driverless Industrial Trucks and Their Systems. International Organization for Standardization.

15.5.2 Physical disturbances versus digital input perturbations

A vision model that survives synthetic pixel noise injected into an input tensor can still go blind when low-angle warehouse glare saturates the physical camera lens and its auto-exposure loop. Digital perturbations and physical disturbances challenge an embodied machine at different interfaces. Synthetic tensor noise tests how sensitive a network’s decision boundary is to small numerical shifts in representation space while bypassing the physical sensor altogether. An optical disturbance, by contrast, exercises the lens, coatings, auto-exposure and gain control, demosaicing, and camera interface timing. Neither test substitutes for the other, so a threat model must state whether an injected fault corrupts digital packets in flight, perturbs the physical environment, or hits both at once.

Evaluating the rack-end safety claim requires injecting realistic physical disturbances: stray illumination and direct glare that blind camera sensors, mechanical chassis vibration that corrupts inertial measurements, and floor transitions that slash available braking traction. Every physical injection requires a repeatable physical actuator and an independent ground-truth witness. For instance, glare injection requires calibrated illumination measurements beside the lens together with the declared optical geometry, spectrum, and temporal waveform. Chassis vibration requires external optical motion capture independent of onboard wheel odometry. Floor variations require standardized friction-measurement drag sleds. In cyber-physical verification, receiving a syntactically valid sensor packet with correct checksums over an internal bus confirms only that the digital transmission succeeded; it provides no guarantee that the estimated state accurately reflects physical reality.

15.5.3 What the envelope claim leaves open

Passing the initial F1 trial at one speed and arm posture still leaves neighboring speeds, higher centers of gravity, and worst-case injection timings untested. That first diagnostic run suppresses trajectory renewal while the base is in motion and measures the deceleration-onset timestamp and stopping distance. Subsequent falsification campaigns systematically vary initial velocities, arm configurations, and injection phases to hunt for minimal operating margins (section 15.9.2). Formal methods, high-fidelity simulation, and physical hardware testing provide complementary evidence, but none can compensate for an unstated physical premise or an unverified operating assumption.

An operating boundary remains defensible only when every parameter carries its physical unit, traceable provenance, calibrated uncertainty, and designated engineering owner. Specifying a credible deceleration of 2 m/s² remains an incomplete assertion until the verification evidence documents its origin. The evidence must show whether it was measured during physical stopping trials at maximum payload mass, extracted from an actuator manufacturer’s datasheet, or computed using a theoretical friction model. It must also give the measurement uncertainty of the external tracking reference. Any hardware modification, whether a new polyurethane wheel tread compound or new motor-drive firmware pushed across the EtherCAT fieldbus, obligates the component owner to re-evaluate whether the operational boundary has shifted.

Validating an operating envelope requires attacking its boundary surfaces rather than testing comfortable interior states. Evaluating an unloaded base crawling across a dry floor with pristine camera frames proves only that nominal execution works under ideal conditions. Instead, the verification harness distributes test points across three regimes. Points just inside the envelope verify that the control policy preserves closed-loop stability under the largest permissible operational stress. Points on the boundary confirm that safety margins survive nominal sensor noise and compute jitter. Points just outside verify that the safety enforcer reliably preempts the policy, executing a deterministic stop that halts the base within the survival envelope before clearance is exhausted.

A physical hardware malfunction and an algorithmic functional insufficiency require different verification strategies. A stereo camera can transmit uncorrupted, on-time video packets over PCIe while ambient glare blinds its depth estimation. A complete campaign must therefore inject faults into both the physical execution mechanisms and the sensory streams on which those mechanisms rely. Adding an enforcer module does not let it observe what its sensors cannot measure; when physical sensing cannot distinguish safe states from hazardous ones, the architect must restrict the operating envelope to conditions where safety can be enforced. Once every timing constraint, latency budget, and environmental threshold of that envelope is specified in physical units, the design claims from earlier chapters become concrete targets for fault injection, each paired with a boundary threshold that must trigger protective intervention.

Systems Perspective 15.2: The hardware falsification principle
A hardware-dependent safety claim needs an observable challenge to its physical premises. If the campaign cannot inject the relevant disturbance or retain the required witness, the record must preserve that verification gap.

15.6 Deriving the Fault List

The proposer stall fault (F1) originates from negating an operational claim: that proposal renewal proceeds reliably within its granted lease. Deriving a comprehensive fault list, however, requires two complementary methodologies. Inverting design records tests the explicit assumptions codified across the system architecture. Working forward from the physical realities of mechanics, power networks, and human interaction exposes the hazards and unmodeled edge cases that software design records routinely omit. Human interaction includes who is in view. A fault list that seeds only people standing upright leaves untested the kneeling, carrying, and seated people a learned detector may see later, so the groups of people the machine must detect enter the list as a coverage dimension of their own.

↪ How many people of each group a test campaign needs before its detection estimate can resolve the gap that matters is developed in Sample sizes for each group.

15.6.1 Inverting design records and the five-element chain

If the machine’s specification claims that the chunk policy renews its trajectory proposal every 50 ms at every speed up to the 1.5 m/s drive limit, the verification suite turns that claim inside out. It injects stalled renewals, delayed observations, and out-of-range encoder readings to see whether the permission path catches the violation in time. Every preceding chapter supplies claims that can be inverted in the same way. Physical Data through Closed-Loop Evaluation state the learned policy’s training distribution, state-action assumptions, and inference latency ceilings. Sensor Perception through Workload Placement state bus allocations, observation freshness, and estimation bounds. Supervisory Intervention states how quickly authority leaves a failing policy and which subsystem takes over.

Where the five-part test contract of section 15.1 states what makes any trial valid evidence, a five-element chain states how a single inverted claim is built into such a trial. Its first element supplies the contract’s bounded claim, its last element supplies the contract’s oracle, and the three between them specify the mechanism the trial exercises:

  1. The negated condition: the exact architectural or timing invariant violated (for example, proposal renewal halted after lease issue);
  2. The physical injection point: the exact hardware or bus interface where the disturbance enters (for example, an inline field-programmable gate array (FPGA) serializer, CAN FD disturbance node, or programmable DC load);
  3. The expected detector: the deterministic monitoring primitive tasked with catching the violation (for example, a lease countdown timer or evidence-epoch comparator);
  4. The bounded response: the deterministic transition executed by the enforcer (for example, revoking proposal authority and commanding the resident stop suffix); and
  5. The observable pass condition: the quantitative, independently witnessed metric that scores the trial (for F1 at aisle speed, onset \(\le\) 82 ms and travel from renewal \(\le\) 751.9 mm).

A recorded design property becomes a testable fault only when all five elements are fully defined and observable. If any element of this chain cannot be defined or measured, the architecture has a verification gap: the underlying claim cannot be falsified by empirical test. Systems engineers cannot ignore such gaps; resolving them requires dedicated hardware instrumentation (such as external optical trackers or non-intrusive trace macros), a narrower operational design domain, or a structural redesign of the execution path.

Inversion also applies to the learned proposer’s implicit assumptions, where distribution shift can induce functional insufficiency even when every observation frame arrives intact and on time. For example, a chunk policy trained in simulation on dry concrete with friction coefficient 0.60 proposes decelerations that an inspected floor at 0.12 turns into wheel slip. Inverting that evaluation claim requires injecting degraded traction conditions at the physical contact patch. Sensor bias, calibration drift, and input perturbations are injected for the same reason: a policy with high nominal reward can still command high-frequency actuator chatter once its inputs leave the training distribution. Under these conditions, an overly permissive execution path can destabilize the plant while executing syntactically valid commands, demonstrating that software commands and mechanical dynamics remain tightly coupled across the causal boundary.

At each inter-module contract, the fault list negates architectural guarantees directly at their physical handoff points: observation freshness, state validity, intent lifetime, planning deadlines, enforcement independence, and resource allocation. For the 60 ms chunk lease, the test harness replays the expired admitted record 65 ms after its last valid renewal, verifying that the permission path firmly refuses to extend execution authority. A concurrent proposal represents an entirely separate admission attempt: it must satisfy fresh evidence, kinematic state validity, mode consistency, and stopping-reserve checks before receiving a lease. To test the enforcer’s claimed independence from the policy processor on the selected discrete placement, the verification suite saturates the application processor’s memory interconnect with DMA traffic while independently timing the safety microcontroller. Host contention may delay proposal delivery and age its supporting evidence, but the enforcer must still satisfy its 400 μs execution deadline within every 1 ms control tick. This rigorous inversion prevents arbitrary over-testing: bus contention, clock skew, and supply fluctuations are injected precisely where the architecture claims tolerance, rather than drawing from generic fault menus that impose requirements the machine was never specified to meet.

Inversion has a structural limit: it surfaces only faults rooted in assumptions the design team explicitly wrote down. The resulting fault list is therefore only as comprehensive as the recorded architectural claims. If no engineering record documents how the base’s spring-applied brake behaves when its friction lining glazes under repeated emergency stops, inversion cannot synthesize the failure mode, and the hazard never reaches the test suite.

15.6.2 Forward physical analysis and common-cause modes

Drive wheels skid when commanded deceleration exceeds the traction limit of the contact patch. Brake linings glaze under repeated thermal cycles. Solid tires wear down and alter the rolling radius that wheel odometry relies on. Structural vibration gradually loosens camera mounts until spatial calibration drifts. Because these hazards arise from contact mechanics, heat, and wear rather than software bugs, no software abstraction represents them, and inverting design records will not uncover them. A second, independent fault source therefore works forward from the physical machine (its mechanical body, power distribution network, floor interface, and operating environment). It uses Nancy Leveson’s Systems-Theoretic Accident Model and Processes (STAMP) framework5 (Leveson 2011) to trace how physical and control interactions produce hazards even when no software module has crashed.

5 STAMP and STPA: Leveson (2004) proposed STAMP, which treats safety as a dynamic control problem rather than a component-reliability problem, recognizing that catastrophic accidents often arise from unsafe interactions among components that each operate strictly within specification. Leveson (2011) established the Systems-Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA) hazard-analysis method on this foundation, identifying unsafe control actions at subsystem interfaces, such as an integration seam where bus communication latency or digital filter lag erodes phase margin. Because no individual component has failed at such a seam, its unsafe control actions supply critical fault modes that inverting nominal design records cannot expose.

Leveson, Nancy. 2004. “A New Accident Model for Engineering Safer Systems.” Safety Science 42 (4): 237–70. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0925-7535(03)00047-X.
Leveson, Nancy. 2011. Engineering a Safer World: Systems Thinking Applied to Safety. MIT Press.

Consider a motor drive that continues producing positive torque after receiving a stop command. A shorted inverter switch, welded contactor, or stuck gate-driver enable line can defeat the normal powered stopping trajectory. The test harness must independently observe delivered phase torque, rotor velocity, and the engagement of the secondary isolation path. While Safe Torque Off (STO) inhibits torque generation without providing electrical isolation, it cannot enforce nominal stopping distances on low-friction floors or prevent back-driving on gravity-loaded articulated joints. The safety architecture must specify a separately justified containment response alongside an independent physical measurement oracle. If no such path exists, the fault exposes an architectural omission rather than an operational delay that can borrow from the normal stopping budget.

↩︎ Safe Torque Off and the industrial stop categories are established in Industrial stop categories and the safe torque off trap.

Working forward exposes hardware fault classes that software design records rarely imply: loss of reference clocks, power supply brownouts that leave digital logic in indeterminate voltage states, pulse-width modulation (PWM) inverter switching noise coupling into differential encoder lines, corrupted non-volatile configuration registers, and reset lines that bridge domains intended to remain galvanically or logically isolated. For instance, if the safety microcontroller and the high-performance application processor share a single power-management integrated circuit (PMIC), a transient voltage droop during a sudden drive-current surge can reset both processors simultaneously just as the learned proposer loses trajectory tracking. A software interface model that assumes an ideal DC power supply omits this failure. An enforcer cannot arrest an errant policy if it loses operating power alongside the workload it supervises; the deterministic safety runtime must outlive the faulted workload using independently qualified execution and power resources. The running machine uses an isolated, capacitor-backed auxiliary supply to survive the declared supply fault.

Forward physical analysis likewise evaluates the architecture’s common-cause independence claims. Subsystems that appear strictly isolated on an architectural block diagram frequently share physical vulnerabilities. For example, the primary lidar communication lines and redundant safety bumper wiring may share a single mechanical cable carrier subject to pinch wear, depend on a shared analog voltage reference inside a multichannel converter, or rely on timer silicon containing identical silicon errata. An electrical transient that latches up both channels simultaneously destroys any safety argument predicated on statistical independence.
