Common-cause analysis traces harness routing, power distribution paths, and clock distribution trees to identify shared failure paths and justify independence against the declared fault set, rather than assume separation from the block diagram.

↩︎ The independence claims for the permission path’s execution unit, oscillator, power rail, and memory are established in Hardware Isolation and Fault Containment.

This physical analysis extends directly to human error and foreseeable operational misuse. Warehouse personnel may jumper an optical rack-end sensor during maintenance, leave a manual pallet jack parked across an automated transit corridor, or unplug an acoustic warning beacon to silence workplace noise, while remote teleoperators may request transit speeds exceeding local aisle restrictions. When a remote assistant commands the vehicle’s full 1.5 m/s drive limit within a narrow aisle, the verification suite must confirm that the arbiter denies the command by capability constraint or clamps it to the admitted manual mode speed under an active manual_drive state. Furthermore, the deterministic permission check must evaluate the assistant’s command with the same scrutiny applied to autonomous proposals.

Working forward from the physical plant adds a representative mechanical failure mode, the actuator jam, to the record-derived inventory in table 2, though full verification still requires a plant-specific hazard analysis tailored to the vehicle’s mechanical kinematics and operational envelope. Alongside inverted design claims and forward physical hazards, the fault suite in table 2 also evaluates the four authority transition cases (A1–A4) established in Per-tick evidence tuples and the fault injection suite under the operating state that makes intervention most urgent: a fully loaded base approaching the rack end at velocity. The test campaign injects takeover requests at two speeds to distinguish the candidate moving transition from a request the clearance screen rejects; the operative design stops before handing over. At the 0.75 m/s slow-down, the partial-budget waiting margin is 751.6 ms, enough for the 605 ms in-loop takeover and the 63.3 ms dispatch-to-admission allocation together. Clearance-derived fallback deadlines therefore identifies a candidate moving in-loop handover for qualification, subject to the complete session budget and live stopping checks. The trial must show that a handover left pending still ends in a local stop by its deadline. At the full 1.3 m/s aisle speed the margin is 100.8 ms, only 37.5 ms once the allocation is charged, which cannot hold the takeover, so the arbiter must reject the moving handover and dispatch a local stop before the deadline expires. An out-of-loop takeover begins only after a stop, from standstill, in an admitted mode. Evaluating either sequence solely while the vehicle is parked proves nothing about dynamic performance; the trial must demonstrate that rejection, timeout, and braking occur with bounded latency while the base moves.

15.6.3 Physical sensor spoofing and proposer injection

Whether sun glare washes out a camera lens by accident or an attacker shines a laser into the optics on purpose, the camera interface delivers a well-formed, checksum-valid packet whose physical reading is false. In both ambient disturbances and transduction-layer cyber-physical attacks, checksum validation and bus integrity checks pass cleanly while the robot’s view of the aisle is corrupted. When evaluating such an optical disturbance (retroreflective glare, strobed lighting, or lens occlusion), the test protocol must specify the illumination levels and vehicle approach vectors that challenge obstacle detection, the cross-sensor plausibility checks tasked with identifying the discrepancy, and the deterministic fallback maneuver. An independent ground-truth optical tracking system monitors obstacle clearance and base trajectory while camera streams are disturbed. The verification record must distinguish an actively detected anomaly followed by a controlled deceleration from a missed obstacle where collision was avoided purely by chance. If a monitoring subsystem cannot distinguish corrupted sensor data from valid physical evidence, that operational scenario constitutes a verification gap; documenting an aspirational safety monitor in an architecture diagram does not ensure it will fire under physical stress.

The learned proposer is itself a primary attack surface; targeting it tests the proposal boundary far more directly than conventional bus or memory faults. Because a vision-language-action (VLA) model consumes natural language instructions and camera frames directly, an adversary can manipulate the model’s outputs through either modality without touching a physical wire or intercepting a bus. Robey and colleagues demonstrated automated prompt generation that induced robots controlled by language models, including a commercial quadruped evaluated strictly as a black box, to execute harmful physical actions across diverse operational settings despite high-level safety guardrails (Robey et al. 2025). On the visual side, Wang and colleagues showed that introducing a compact adversarial patch6 into a VLA’s camera field of view could degrade simulated manipulation success rates to zero (Wang et al. 2025). On a deployed warehouse machine, physical injection points encompass task instruction strings, floor placards, shipping container barcodes, or printed warning signs within the camera’s visual cone.

Robey, Alexander, Zachary Ravichandran, Vijay Kumar, Hamed Hassani, and George J. Pappas. 2025. “Jailbreaking LLM-Controlled Robots.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 11948–56. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA55743.2025.11128119.

6 Adversarial Patch: Brown et al. (2017) demonstrated that printing an image-independent physical patch and placing it within a visual scene forces an image classifier to ignore surrounding objects and predict the attacker’s target class. The perturbation enters entirely through the optical transducer without requiring software access, bus interception, or packet injection. In an embodied system, this attack operates wholly on the proposer’s side of the proposal boundary, where the permission path can constrain the physical consequences of an altered command but cannot observe the visual artifact that induced it.

Brown, Tom B., Dandelion Mané, Aurko Roy, Martín Abadi, and Justin Gilmer. 2017. “Adversarial Patch.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1712.09665.
Wang, Taowen, Cheng Han, James Chenhao Liang, Wenhao Yang, Dongfang Liu, Luna Xinyu Zhang, Qifan Wang, Jiebo Luo, and Ruixiang Tang. 2025. “Exploring the Adversarial Vulnerabilities of Vision-Language-Action Models in Robotics.” IEEE/CVF International Conference on Computer Vision (ICCV), 6948–58. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICCV51701.2025.00653.

In semantic target grounding (equation 10.3), the intent model uses a conformal prediction set \(\ConformalPredictionSet\) calibrated to a marginal miscoverage rate \(\ConformalMiscoverageRate\) to filter ambiguous hypotheses by requiring a singleton output (\(|\ConformalPredictionSet| = 1\)). In an adversarial or out-of-distribution physical setting, however, an adversarial patch or optical distortion violates the exchangeability premise of the conformal calibration. An attacker can force an erroneous candidate to pass as a singleton (\(|\ConformalPredictionSet| = 1\)), or the model can misclassify within its nominal \(\ConformalMiscoverageRate\) error budget. Because the deterministic permission path does not parse task instructions or inspect the conformal set \(\ConformalPredictionSet\), it cannot distinguish an adversarial proposal from an authentic user command. Instead, under its qualified measurement and timing premises, the enforcer rejects or modifies an injected proposal when the requested physical action violates its monitored constraints: velocity and torque envelopes, dynamic stopping reserves, swept-volume occupancy, evidence freshness, or active lease duration. If an adversarial proposal meets these physical constraints and the authority checks, the enforcer may admit it, even if the robot picks an unintended bin, deposits cargo in an unauthorized clear aisle, or actuates an access gate prematurely. The permission path supports physical containment and dynamic feasibility under its declared premises; those checks do not establish semantic task correctness. This operational asymmetry highlights why systems architects decouple proposal synthesis from safety enforcement: protective safety guarantees must hold regardless of how an action was synthesized, while task-level semantic fidelity cannot be guaranteed by low-level enforcers. Consequently, the proposer-injection row in table 2 evaluates trials solely against independent physical oracles, recording uncommanded actions that remain within the admissible safety envelope as semantic coverage deficits rather than as safety violations.

↩︎ The limit that the permission path enforces physical feasibility rather than semantic task correctness is established in Intent Grounding Failure Modes and Their Checks.

15.6.4 Choosing the injection phase

Injecting a proposer stall immediately after a lease renewal forces the base to coast on open-loop setpoints for nearly the full lease duration, stressing tracking error and clearance. Injecting the same stall microseconds before lease expiration, by contrast, catches the arbiter right at the boundary between renewal and revocation. Because the instant a fault arrives within the control cycle changes which hardware and software paths bear the load, a verification campaign cannot treat repeated fault injections at arbitrary times as interchangeable trials. Recording the exact injection phase relative to the control tick turns phase into a controlled test variable. Sweeping across injection phase, fault duration, and disturbance magnitude exposes race conditions in the arbitration logic and supplies the signed safety margins that automated falsification algorithms search over (section 15.9.2).

15.6.5 Compound faults and coupled disturbances

When a drive-current surge sags a shared DC power rail, it does not wait for the perception pipeline or the safety microcontroller to finish its current cycle: a single electrical dip can brown out the application processor, freeze an image serializer, and jitter a shared clock all at once. While each row of table 2 tests one disturbance in isolation, real machines rarely fail one subsystem at a time. Combining rows at random would cause a combinatorial explosion without targeting realistic failures, so the campaign instead derives compound faults from shared hardware causes. The placement record (The Placement Record) lists every physical resource that the permission path shares with the learned workloads, and each shared power rail, clock tree, or communication bus becomes a compound fault injected at the shared hardware root rather than at downstream software symptoms.

On the mobile manipulator, the primary shared vulnerability is the main DC drive bus, which powers the traction motor drives directly while feeding the application processor through an intermediate DC-DC converter. Meanwhile, the safety-critical permission path operates from an isolated, capacitor-backed hold-up rail. Notebook 15.2 evaluates the electrical headroom and timing budgets for both F4 fault injection trials using the vehicle’s electrical specifications.

Napkin Math 15.2: The supply headroom
Problem: At the depleted point, can the drive transient alone carry the shared bus below the lockout of the converter that feeds the application processor, and how much of the permission rail’s hold-up does the longest stop in the optimistic screening branch spend?

Variables:

  • Depleted bus \(\DepletedBusVoltage =\) 42 V and converter lockout \(\ConverterLockoutVoltage =\) 33 V (Datasheet).
  • Source and harness resistance \(\ElectricalResistance =\) 60 mΩ (Illustrative; needs a measured value) and drive transient \(\ConductorCurrent =\) 70 A (Illustrative).
  • Rail hold-up 2 s (Chosen) and spring-brake engagement \(\SpringBrakeEngagementTime =\) 80 ms (Illustrative).
  • Longest stop in the optimistic all-weight screen: from the 1.23 m/s inspected-floor ceiling at \(\AvailableDeceleration =\) 1.18 m/s² (Derived from floor friction with the entire weight on driven wheels) with \(\JerkLimit =\) 7.11 m/s³ (Derived).

Math:

F4a, sag under the transient and the step that reaches lockout. \[V_{\min} = V_{\text{low}} - I\,R = 42 - 70 \times 0.060 = 37.8\ \text{V}, \qquad I_{\text{cross}} = \frac{V_{\text{low}} - V_{\text{UVLO}}}{R} = \frac{42 - 33}{0.060} = 150\ \text{A}\]

F4b, flag to brakes set, with the stop time of equation 11.17. \[t_{\text{set}} = (T_{\text{tick}} + T_{\text{bus}} + T_{\text{act}}) + \left(\frac{v}{a} + \frac{a}{j_{\max}}\right) + t_{\text{engage}} = 22 + \left(\frac{1.231}{1.177} + \frac{1.177}{7.11}\right)\text{s} + 80 = 22 + 1045.9 + 165.5 + 80 = 1313.4\ \text{ms}\]

Result: The transient leaves 4.8 V of headroom above the lockout, and reaching it takes a 150 A step, more than twice the transient. The 2 s hold-up outlasts the 1313.43 ms from flag to brakes set by 686.6 ms.

Systems insight: Neither trial can rest on the commanded load. F4a must step the supply itself, because the drive’s own current cannot reach the lockout, and F4b must open the feed during the longest stop admitted by the qualified loaded envelope, since a shorter stop passes on hold-up the claim never needed. The computed duration belongs to the optimistic all-weight screen; loaded qualification must replace its braking premise before fixing the physical F4b oracle. The headroom rests on an illustrative resistance and would vanish near 129 mΩ, about twice that value, so the source resistance measured under load is the first number the supply campaign must supply.

A compound fault trial must capture high-bandwidth electrical waveforms while independently recording the behavior of the learned policy, perception pipelines, motor controllers, and external telemetry witnesses. Engineers cannot infer that processors reset or sensor frames froze from software logs alone, because the electrical disturbance often corrupts the operating system writing the log. For example, a voltage droop on a shared power rail can induce oscillator jitter that slows the enforcer’s control cycle, freeze an image serializer so that a camera publishes identical frames with plausible timestamps, and reset an external flight recorder during the exact same interval. Similarly, electromagnetic burst interference across a shared fieldbus ages perceptual observations while stalling the permission path’s read requests and suppressing the diagnostic heartbeats that would otherwise signal the delay. A drift or jitter transient in a shared reference clock corrupts timestamps across the proposal-enforcement boundary at the same time, allowing stale data to pass as fresh evidence. The physical oracle evaluating a compound fault must therefore verify both that the protective response triggered within its deadline and that the witness instruments and evidence logs remained intact throughout the event (section 15.12).

Because unmodeled physical couplings cannot be found from a resource inventory alone, forward physical analysis must always complement derivation from the inventory. Together, the resulting fault catalog unites four sources: inverted design contracts, forward-derived physical hazards, the authority transfer cases (A1–A4), and compound disturbances on shared hardware. Defining this catalog establishes what faults the machine must survive; choosing the physical fidelity of the test bench determines which of those claims a given trial can actually test.

15.7 The Qualification Ladder

A desktop simulator can stall the proposer of F1 across every combination of speed and payload overnight, but it cannot sag the drive bus of F4. The simulated power network has no buck-boost converter to lock out and no microcontroller to brown out and reset, so a million passing rollout assertions (assert clearance >= 0) there say nothing about F4. That is the digital twin delusion of section 15.1, and the remaining question is where, precisely, a given simulator stops being evidence.

The gap between simulation and reality stems directly from what the simulator abstracts away. When a physics simulation omits embedded target execution and electrical dynamics, it relies on five pervasive idealizations, each of which must be evaluated against the specific claim under test:

  1. Computation takes zero virtual time: The simulator pauses its simulation clock while the neural policy executes. This treats heavy neural forward passes as instantaneous, completely decoupling inference latency from physical base velocity (\(\BlindTravelDistance = \SpeedAtDetection \PrebrakeDelay\)).
  2. Memory bandwidth is infinite: Tensors pass across shared in-memory references. This completely omits the shared-memory contention, cache evictions, and DMA serialization stalls that govern real-time execution on an edge SoC.
  3. Power rails are infinitely stiff: Power supplies are modeled as idealized constant-voltage sources (\(\DcBusVoltage = \text{const}\)). This ignores the resistive droop (\(\FiniteChange \DcBusVoltage = \ConductorCurrent \ElectricalResistance\)) of the drive bus under sudden current steps (sized for this machine in notebook 15.2), the inductive flyback spikes from sharp current transients, and the resulting brownout resets.
  4. Clocks are perfectly synchronized: The simulation advances monotonically in fixed discrete time-steps (\(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\)). This eliminates asynchronous clock drift, interrupt jitter, and transceiver arbitration races between the neural host and the real-time safety microcontroller.
  5. Contact mechanics are simplified: Numerical contact solvers approximate Coulomb friction with linearized friction cones or convex relaxations that permit artificial creep under static load (Todorov 2014). They routinely neglect the transition between static and kinetic friction, dynamic tire slip, and brake-lining glazing during emergency stops.
Todorov, Emanuel. 2014. “Convex and Analytically-Invertible Dynamics with Contacts and Constraints: Theory and Implementation in MuJoCo.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 6054–61. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA.2014.6907751.

↩︎ Resistive droop on a shared drive bus and the brownout resets it causes are established in Power distribution impedance, transient droop, and brownout resets.

Each qualification environment closes, breaks, or substitutes different links in the chain connecting sensing, inference, communication buses, motor drives, and physical mechanisms. The qualification ladder orders four verification environments by how much of the cyber-physical loop is physically executed rather than simulated: software-in-the-loop, processor-in-the-loop (PIL), hardware-in-the-loop (HIL), and physical fault rigs. Software-in-the-loop is the lowest rung and the physical rig the highest, so climbing the ladder means making more of the loop physical. As physical hardware replaces numerical models, test fidelity rises, but trial throughput drops by orders of magnitude. This progression is not, however, an evidentiary hierarchy in which higher rungs subsume lower ones. Each rung yields a distinct category of empirical evidence while remaining blind to physical phenomena outside its boundary. A million successful software-in-the-loop rollouts reveal nothing about bus contention, DMA stalls, clock drift, or supply rail sag on the embedded hardware.

15.7.1 What each of the four rungs makes physical

On the warehouse mobile manipulator, software-in-the-loop evidence terminates at the boundary of the numerical plant model; a mechanical brake modeled merely as a first-order lag cannot capture pad thermal glazing, wheel slip, or torque saturation. Processor-in-the-loop (PIL) execution introduces the cross-compiled binary running on the actual target silicon (such as an ARM Cortex-R or RISC-V safety core), validating fixed-point quantization, instruction timing, and stack headroom. Hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) benches mount the two-processor architecture, with its physical fieldbus, power regulators, and motor drive stages, against a hard real-time FPGA plant model or an instrumented dynamometer. Finally, physical fault rigs close the full loop with the production chassis, mechanical brakes, wire harness, and physical warehouse floor.

↩︎ The two-processor architecture, which runs the permission path on a safety microcontroller apart from the learned proposer’s application processor, is established in The Independence Requirement and the Two-Processor Hypothesis.

Two operational testing paradigms exist alongside this qualification ladder, but both break the closed loop entirely. Trace replay feeds recorded sensor streams into the candidate controller; while it preserves authentic sensor noise and optical artifacts, the recorded frames reflect the actions of the original historical policy rather than reacting to the candidate’s new commands. Shadow operation runs the candidate model in parallel with an incumbent, validated controller. If the candidate proposes an aggressive velocity vector that would drive the base into a storage rack, the logger records the telemetry while the incumbent controller safely halts the base. Because neither approach permits candidate actions to alter future state observations, neither reveals the closed-loop physical consequences of an erroneous command.

A production shadow deployment cannot allow a stalled proposer to drive toward a human worker at a rack end to see if the brakes engage in time. A software simulator cannot expose a timing race between transceiver silicon buffers. An instrumented physical dynamometer cannot execute the tens of millions of randomized runs required to bound extreme distribution tails. Systems engineers must therefore route each fault injection scenario to the specific rung on the ladder that embodies its underlying physical and causal mechanism. Coverage of the causal loop grows with each higher rung, from decision logic alone to drives and floor contact, while trial throughput shrinks, so no rung dominates the others (figure 2).

Selecting the proper qualification rung requires identifying the exact premise that a fault challenges. Validating the software lease expiry under CPU contention in F1 requires target silicon execution to capture true processor scheduling interference. In contrast, evaluating supply rail droop in F4 demands the production power distribution network and independent oscilloscope instrumentation across the DC bus. Similarly, certifying a stopping-distance budget requires a fully ballasted physical chassis operating over a qualified floor surface. A sound verification strategy uses high-throughput simulation to search the combinatorial operational space for edge cases, then reproduces those critical scenarios on the specific hardware rung that contains the physical mechanism under test. Agreement between two rungs validates only the shared causal links common to both; table 3 outlines these strict evidentiary boundaries.

15.7.2 Where each rung’s simulation boundary cuts the loop

Replacing physical wire harnesses, ground returns, and distributed actuator nodes with desktop simulations strips away inductive voltage droop, transmission reflections, and electromagnetic interference (EMI). Every qualification rung has a simulation boundary, the interface where numerical models, emulated buses, or replayed traces sever the exchange of energy and information between computation and mechanics, and no environment can certify properties that depend on physics outside that boundary. Software-in-the-loop testing falsifies algorithmic decision logic and state-machine transitions across broad parametric sweeps. PIL uncovers numerical divergences from cross-compilation and profiles worst-case execution time on target silicon, yet because it funnels input and output over a hardware debug interface (such as a JTAG port or serial UART link), it cannot stress physical communication buses. HIL brings in the true physical transceivers, bus contention, and electrical transients, while leaving contact mechanics to real-time models. Finally, physical fault rigs subject the structural plant to unmodeled Coulomb friction, tire deformation, and thermal dissipation.

A higher rung therefore does not subsume or obsolete the lower ones. Verification is an exercise in constrained optimization across a Pareto frontier: parallel software-in-the-loop clusters explore millions of permutations of obstacle layouts and lighting variations. Physical rigs, by contrast, capture true contact mechanics at the expense of manual resets, mechanical wear, and thermal recovery between runs. Each stage occupies a unique operating point balancing combinatorial test throughput against physical causal fidelity. A mechanism omitted from a model cannot fail within it. If a simulator omits Coulomb breakaway friction and stator inductive lag, a simulated robot joint moves immediately upon receiving a torque command. On real hardware, however, that same joint remains stationary during a stiction dead time before lagging behind the commanded trajectory. This is a classic manifestation of the omitted-mechanism gap, which no amount of software simulation can uncover.

To guard against false confidence, every empirical verification claim must explicitly catalog its causal envelope: which links in the sensing-computation-actuation loop were physically real, which were simulated, which were replayed from static traces, which were bypassed, and which remained unobserved. Stating that the safety permission path halted the mobile base within 969.0 mm is meaningless in isolation. An engineer must specify whether the wheel-speed velocity signal was computed by a numerical ODE solver, streamed from a physical optical encoder over a saturated CAN bus, or read out from an in-memory replay buffer. Mapping this boundary exposes the physical assumptions behind each pass and prevents latent hardware hazards from hiding behind passing software assertions.

15.7.3 Scope of a formal verification result

A formal proof that a permission filter preserves safety over a mathematical plant model still cannot prevent a crash if the tires slip on a wet floor, the camera frames arrive late, or the proof is asked to cover a vision-language-action proposer fifty times larger than any network a complete verifier can solve. Formal proof provides a fifth source of evidence alongside the physical qualification ladder, but a proof holds only inside its mathematical model of plant states, sensor noise, and bounded disturbances. Translating that guarantee to the embodied machine still rests on empirical measurements of plant dynamics, observation deadlines, bus latencies, and actuator torque limits. Which components of the running machine a proof can reach at all depends on the current scale limits of neural-network verification.

Tools for formal neural-network verification operate in one of two regimes: open loop or closed loop. An open-loop verifier checks an open-loop property: given any input in a specified bounded set (for example, every camera frame within an \(\ell_\infty\) ball around a nominal image, meaning every frame whose pixels each lie within \(\AdversarialPerturbationBudget\) of the nominal values), the network’s output is guaranteed to satisfy a declared constraint. Two algorithmic primitives drive these tools:

  • Bound propagation pushes the input set forward through the network layer by layer. By replacing each nonlinear activation with linear relaxation bounds, it computes a sound, conservative bounding box around the outputs. If this enclosure satisfies the property, the proof succeeds immediately.
  • When bound propagation leaves property satisfaction ambiguous, branch and bound partitions the input space or splits ambiguous piecewise-linear activations into distinct linear regimes, recomputing bounds over each sub-domain. For supported network and property classes, a complete search can decide the property in principle, but it scales exponentially with the number of split activations in the worst case; a timeout or unknown result is not a proof. Solvers such as Reluplex (Katz et al. 2017) and Marabou (Wu et al. 2024) formulate these activation states as satisfiability modulo theories (SMT) or mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) problems, whereas the α,β-CROWN verifier accelerates bound propagation and branch-and-bound exploration directly on GPUs (Xu et al. 2021; Wang et al. 2021).
Xu, Kaidi, Huan Zhang, Shiqi Wang, Yihan Wang, Suman Jana, Xue Lin, and Cho-Jui Hsieh. 2021. “Fast and Complete: Enabling Complete Neural Network Verification with Rapid and Massively Parallel Incomplete Verifiers.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Ivanov, Radoslav, James Weimer, Rajeev Alur, George J. Pappas, and Insup Lee. 2019. “Verisig: Verifying Safety Properties of Hybrid Systems with Neural Network Controllers.” Proceedings of the 22nd ACM International Conference on Hybrid Systems: Computation and Control (HSCC), 169–78. https://doi.org/10.1145/3302504.3311806.
Mitchell, Ian M., Alexandre M. Bayen, and Claire J. Tomlin. 2005. “A Time-Dependent Hamilton-Jacobi Formulation of Reachable Sets for Continuous Dynamic Games.” IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control 50 (7): 947–57. https://doi.org/10.1109/TAC.2005.851439.

Verifying a closed-loop property poses a far steeper challenge. Rather than checking a static input-output mapping, closed-loop verification asks whether the continuous state of a physical plant driven by a neural policy remains within a safe invariant set over an extended time horizon. To answer this, reachability analysis evaluates the continuous trajectories of the physical plant under bounded disturbances. Forward reachability tools, such as NNV, Verisig, and POLAR, compose the network’s output bounding box with the differential equations of the physical plant at each discrete control step, computing forward reachable sets for compact controllers on low-dimensional dynamical systems (Tran et al. 2020; Ivanov et al. 2019; Huang et al. 2022). Backward reachability via Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) reachability analysis instead frames safety as a zero-sum differential game between the control policy and worst-case environmental disturbances. It computes the exact maximal safe invariant set as the zero level set of an optimal control value function (Hamilton-Jacobi reachability and worst-case differential games) (Mitchell et al. 2005; Bansal et al. 2017). While Hamilton-Jacobi reachability provides non-conservative safety certificates for continuous nonlinear dynamics, solving the underlying Hamilton-Jacobi-Isaacs partial differential equation on a Eulerian grid scales exponentially with continuous state dimension. Consequently, Hamilton-Jacobi reachability is computationally tractable for decoupled, low-dimensional subsystems (such as a single revolute joint or longitudinal base stopping), whereas forward reachability tools struggle with the rapid accumulation of over-approximation error across long planning horizons. NNV also supports bounded image-input robustness analysis through ImageStars (Tran et al. 2020), but those image results do not establish an end-to-end closed-loop guarantee for the running machine’s high-resolution vision-language-action stream. Its permission path instead exposes a handful of calibrated physical states and a declared stopping invariant (Safety Enforcement).

Table 4 benchmarks the state of the art in formal verification. In the 2025 International Verification of Neural Networks Competition (VNN-COMP 2025), the largest scored benchmark network contained 138M parameters, with the vast majority of evaluated models containing fewer than a million parameters (Kaulen et al. 2025). By contrast, the running machine’s intent model, the larger of its two learned proposers, contains 7B parameters, roughly 51× larger than that benchmark outlier. Both proposers also ingest an unconstrained temporal stream of high-resolution camera frames rather than an analytically bounded input set, and a local pixel-perturbation specification alone does not establish stopping safety across changing warehouse scenes and physical states.

The cited results do not establish complete formal verification of this deep proposer under its deployment stream; both computational scale and the absence of a justified visual specification remain barriers. This separation is the third law, proposal is not permission (principle 3), seen from the verifier’s side. The learned proposer is evaluated and stress-tested empirically, and formal proof is reserved for the compact, deterministic permission path, which reads a handful of calibrated physical measurements (wheel speeds, lidar clearances, payload mass) and enforces an analytically tractable stopping invariant that a proof can reach.

↩︎ The separation of a learned proposer from a deterministic permission path is established in The Machine in Five Levels.

Even within the permission path, high-dimensional reachability calculations, nonlinear contact dynamics, and hybrid switching modes can cause mathematical over-approximations to become prohibitively conservative. These practical limits do not render formal analysis useless. Instead, they dictate engineering restraint: they favor designing compact, mathematically transparent permission filters with explicit physical premises that can be verified in minutes, leaving the unmodeled electrical and mechanical dynamics to be systematically challenged on the physical rungs of the qualification ladder.

Checkpoint 15.2: Rung choice and the reach of a proof

A rung, like a proof, supports only the mechanisms it contains.

15.8 Fault Injection on Hardware

A fault injected in simulation tests only the modeled response. On physical machinery, bus contention, microcontroller clock drift, DMA stalls, and transient voltage drops across inductive motor drivers determine whether a safety intervention executes before physical impact. When a simulator fails to capture and calibrate these physical interactions, simulation alone cannot substantiate claims that depend on them. Instead, in-situ fault injection runs directly on the target hardware while an external containment rig keeps the facility safe, deterministically introducing physical, electrical, and computational disturbances (sensor register latches, clock drift, bus contention, supply brownouts, and actuator load stalls) into production silicon, transceivers, timers, shared buses, and mechanics under authentic compute, thermal, and power loads.

15.8.1 Physical injection needles across sensor, bus, and power

A fault injected in software above the driver can exercise downstream responses but does not reproduce a disturbance originating at the physical interface; an empirical verification campaign must place its injectors at the physical boundaries themselves.7 Four complementary injection mechanisms cover the machine’s safety claims:

7 Injection fixtures: Power injectors switch or modulate the supply using solid-state devices, while bus injectors manipulate communication lines through high-impedance buffers. Test instrumentation must remain galvanically isolated from high-current motor transients; otherwise, witness systems will fail alongside the drive. Achievable edge rates and isolation ratings characterize the specific test fixture and must be recorded in the qualification dossier.

  1. Sensor ingress injectors: Inline interposers on camera serial links freeze frames, inject propagation delay while preserving original hardware timestamps, or corrupt pixel payloads at the physical layer without modifying driver software.
  2. Fieldbus disturbance nodes: Inline devices inject protocol-specific faults: corrupted frames, CAN collisions that drive target controllers into bus-off states,8 dropped EtherCAT distributed-clock synchronization frames, or babbling-idiot traffic that monopolizes shared resources.
  3. Supply injectors: Programmable power supplies emulating the battery pack, paired with dynamic electronic loads, step drive-bus and logic rails through calibrated voltage droop profiles. Delivered waveforms must be measured directly at the device terminals, because test-fixture slew-rate limits dictate which high-frequency transients the setup can faithfully reproduce.
  4. Dynamometer actuator injectors: Dynamometer load machines mechanically coupled to wheel hubs or arm joints command calibrated load steps within their measured bandwidth. These challenge responses to increased resistance or abrupt joint loads; reproducing bearing seizures, brake-lining glazing, or joint jams requires mechanism-specific fixtures while instrumentation records the closed-loop motor current response.

8 Bus-Off: The Controller Area Network protocol enforces hardware fault confinement: when a controller’s transmit error count exceeds standard thresholds, it enters bus-off and ceases bus communication until cleared by an idle sequence (International Organization for Standardization 2015). While this prevents a damaged node from corrupting the bus, external disturbance injectors forcing bit collisions can exploit this mechanism to silence healthy nodes without a software fault. Physical fault injection must prove that the safety permission path detects this silenced node and initiates safe fallback within its timing budget.

International Organization for Standardization. 2015. ISO 11898-1:2015: Road Vehicles, Controller Area Network (CAN), Part 1: Data Link Layer and Physical Signalling. International Organization for Standardization.

Verifying the dual-processor safety architecture requires targeted physical injection at the exact hardware boundaries established by the safety contract. Table 2 summarizes the corresponding detectors, recovery actions, and acceptance criteria; the following breakdown details the physical injection mechanics and timing instrumentation for each fault class:

  • Expired Lease Revocation (F1): The test framework permits the chunk policy to issue a single valid renewal, then freezes its task or command output while leaving its periodic heartbeat active, verifying that superficial liveness cannot renew the lease. Dropping subsequent lease messages is a separate test of expiry under communication loss. High-speed logic analyzers time lease expiration, the enforcer’s formal revocation on the subsequent 1 ms control tick, and the mechanical onset of braking across dedicated hardware trigger lines.
  • Stale Observation Injection (F2): An inline FPGA interceptor latches a navigation-camera frame at the image signal processor interface, or suppresses optical encoder updates, while preserving the original hardware timestamp intact. External instrumentation independently records the exact moment of detector trip, the onset of powered electrical deceleration, and the resulting wheel speed profile.
  • Lost-Frame Injection (F3): A fieldbus disturbance node drops or corrupts EtherCAT frames directly at a servo drive’s slave port while the mobile robot cruises. The master’s frame-arrival, validity, and working-counter checks9 detect communication loss under their configured deadlines. At aisle velocity, each lost 1 ms cycle allows the vehicle to travel an unmonitored 1.3 mm, eating directly into the physical safety margin.
  • Drive-Bus Droop and Feed-Loss Injection (F4a, F4b): A programmable supply standing in for the battery pack at its 42 V depleted point delivers the full 70 A transient demanded by aggressive drive acceleration while the application SoC operates at peak load. The supply injector then commands a voltage step that forces the drive bus below the 33 V converter undervoltage lockout, a threshold that operating load alone cannot breach (notebook 15.2). Throughout this transient, the permission path must remain fully operational, sustained by the isolated, held-up rail. An independent digitizer records the permission-path heartbeat, and instrumentation measures the onset of powered deceleration relative to the undervoltage alarm flag. In trial F4b, an electronic switch opens the permission rail’s primary feed during the longest stop admitted by the qualified loaded envelope. The illustrative all-weight screen initiates its resident stop from the 1.23 m/s ceiling. Independent measurements must verify both surviving drive authority and energy absorption throughout dynamic braking. Sustaining logic hold-up alone leaves that premise unverified. Any reset of the permission path constitutes an immediate FAIL in either trial, regardless of stopping distance, because a vehicle that forfeits controlled deceleration to unpowered spring brakes mid-motion violates the deterministic stopping envelope budgeted by the safety contract.
  • Handover Stall Injection (A3): The testbed intentionally freezes operator input while a remote takeover request remains in the handover-pending PEND state defined in Authority states and single-writer actuator ownership, and it drops acknowledgment frames, at both base speeds. At the 0.75 m/s slow-down, a candidate moving handover is tested and any admitted candidate must time out into a local stop. At the 1.3 m/s aisle speed, the handover must be rejected. The operative configuration must stop before granting manual motion at either speed. High-speed probes separately time the enforcer’s fallback dispatch and physical actuation onset at the wheel drives. While the configured waiting allowance limits dispatch latency, physical onset must also satisfy the allocated command-to-response interval.

9 EtherCAT Working Counter: As an on-the-fly datagram traverses each downstream slave, every node that successfully processes assigned process data increments a dedicated working counter field in hardware. The returning counter value informs the master within the exact cycle of transmission how many addressed devices successfully read or wrote their process data. A missing return, an invalid frame, or a working-counter mismatch provides a distinct communication-failure indication. The configured master checks can detect F3 within the cycle’s declared receive deadline, without waiting for a slave drive’s communication watchdog to expire; that detector bound and the subsequent stopping response must be qualified together.

Duplicate writers (A1), tier bypass attempts (A2), and forged or replayed packets (A4) require no specialized mechanical fixtures beyond a secondary bus master and a packet injector configured according to table 2. All four physical injector types and their witness instruments integrate onto a single test bench. The test rig of figure 3 enforces strict physical separation: no witness instrument may share a power rail or a timebase with the target machine under evaluation.

15.8.2 Timing faults across clock domains

Because camera frame capture, trajectory proposal generation, enforcer permission ticks, and fieldbus communication cycles run across asynchronous clock domains (Dating and Placing the Measurement), a fault injected at a fixed offset from the start of a run can miss the narrow alignment where a race condition trips. Reproducible qualification therefore records the disturbance’s phase relative to every relevant clock edge and sweeps across injection phases to expose race conditions that fixed-offset tests miss, while recognizing that empirical phase sweeps alone cannot prove the absence of a worse alignment.

Consider the stale-observation scenario (fault F2) running on production silicon. The navigation camera timestamps each image at the moment of physical exposure, and the perception pipeline budgets a maximum age of 51.6 ms upon trajectory dispatch (The latency waterfall). Because the robot travels at 1.3 m/s, it covers 1.3 mm every millisecond. Unbudgeted perceptual latency drains the stopping distance reserve millisecond by millisecond. The 51.8 ms upper-age bound, however, governs only the dispatch instant; sensory evidence continues to age as the command traverses validation and motor execution. Consequently, the per-tick safety monitor cannot treat the dispatch limit as an open-ended pass; it must evaluate perceptual freshness dynamically against remaining stopping distance and active trajectory validity. The stall that matters is short. Once the dispatch-to-admission allocation is charged, only 37.5 ms of the aisle-speed spare remains (section 15.2), so an undetected stall of that length leaves the machine no clearance to give.

Neither the nominal sensor readout latency from a component datasheet nor the timestamp logged on an isolated image frame proves when the safety enforcer will recognize a stall. Relying on empirical p99 response times provides no safety guarantee because a percentile alone does not bound shared-memory interference; a finite worst-case claim requires an explicit architectural and scheduling model (Response time analysis and execution variance). A defensible containment claim demands an analytic and experimental worst-case detection bound validated across maximum compute saturation, peak fieldbus load, and extreme operating temperatures.

15.8.3 Distributed clock drift and synchronization blackouts

Two processors can each meet every local real-time deadline on their own crystals and still drift apart fast enough during a brief synchronization blackout to let a stale camera frame pass as fresh. The F2 stale-observation detector relies directly on the 0.2 ms clock-conversion bound established in the observation contract of The Observation Contract when translating image capture timestamps into the safety microcontroller’s local timebase. That bound is not an axiomatic constant: Qualifying the claimed error measures it from the tail of the synchronization offset, the path asymmetry, and the drift between synchronization exchanges, and verification asks how much of it survives when those exchanges stop arriving. Consider illustrative oscillator tolerances: ±100 ppm for the application processor crystal, ±50 ppm for the safety microcontroller, and an additional 50 ppm worst-case thermal drift unaccounted for in standard room-temperature ratings. Together, these sources yield a maximum relative drift rate of 200 ppm. At this rate, uncorrected drift alone exhausts the entire 0.2 ms conversion budget in 1 s without active resynchronization. Under healthy operating conditions, periodic synchronization packets arriving every 100 ms confine accumulated drift to 20 μs, leaving the remainder of the error budget for network transit jitter and software conversion overhead.

Suppose electrical noise or packet collisions trigger a synchronization blackout where three consecutive sync frames are dropped, leaving two successful packets separated by 400 ms. Across this four-period blackout window, bounded clock drift alone accumulates to 80 μs. When measured synchronization jitter has a standard deviation of \(\TimingJitter\) = 15 μs, combining the drift rate \(\ClockSynchronizationSkew\) over the blackout duration \(\SynchronizationBlackout\) with a one-sigma jitter excursion yields: \[\BlackoutClockConversionError=\ClockSynchronizationSkew\,\SynchronizationBlackout+\TimingJitter \tag{2}\] totaling 95 μs. The one-sigma estimate in equation 2 represents an illustrative operating point rather than a deterministic upper bound; certifying real-time safety requires bounding tail jitter analytically and stress-testing the conversion path on hardware. If the blackout extends to a fifth period (four consecutive frames dropped), accumulated oscillator drift alone reaches 100 μs before jitter is added, consuming half of the 0.2 ms error budget. The runtime must refuse proposals or enter its qualified fallback before synchronization uncertainty exceeds the conversion bound. If that guard is absent and clock drift or transit jitter breaches the boundary, two failure modes threaten the machine. In the first failure mode, a fresh camera frame appears artificially aged when compared against a fast microcontroller clock; the enforcer rejects valid proposals and initiates an unprompted emergency stop, stranding the vehicle mid-aisle. In the second, more dangerous mode, a stale observation appears artificially fresh against a slow microcontroller clock. The conversion error itself is cheap in distance. At 1.3 mm per millisecond, the whole 0.2 ms bound is worth less than a millimeter of travel. What the error corrupts is the refusal verdict. Because the admission check adds the 0.2 ms conversion bound to the measured timestamp difference to form a conservative upper age, the nominal navigation frame reaches the 51.8 ms refusal threshold exactly at dispatch, so the observation contract of The Observation Contract left that threshold with zero slack. Once a slow microcontroller clock lags by more than the 0.2 ms allowance, the uncompensated clock lag subtracts directly from the measured elapsed age, so the age check can no longer certify that an admitted frame lies within budget. A frame held back by an F2 stall then passes as fresh whenever the uncorrected drift exceeds the stall’s extra age. Because drift keeps accumulating for as long as synchronization stays lost, the stall the check can miss grows with the blackout. The permission path may then admit proposals computed from obstacle coordinates older than the budget allows while the loaded base moves at 1.3 m/s.

A five-period blackout spends half the clock-conversion bound.

Every physical fault injection trial concludes with a rigorous evidentiary burden. The test harness must simultaneously record the trip signal from the internal detector, the precise timestamp of proposal rejection, the actuation takeover at the motor drives, and the final physical stopping trajectory. All measurements must be captured by external, fault-isolated instruments synchronized to a single master reference clock. If a trial fails to record any of these intermediate transitions, it fails to produce actionable verification evidence. Physical fault injection campaigns do not yield simple pass/fail tallies; they generate auditable execution traces binding each trial to its architectural claim, its exact operating envelope, its software build artifacts, and its physical sensor waveforms.

↪ How these trial traces enter a safety case as evidence for a claim is developed in Safety Cases and Claims.

15.9 Searching Beyond the First Trial

A single successful fault trial demonstrates that the safety path can arrest the vehicle under one declared set of operating conditions. Physical machinery, however, rarely fails under tidy nominal conditions; subtle vulnerabilities hide in the narrow corners of the state space where timing jitter, payload shifts, and degraded surface traction align against the enforcer. Exhaustive testing across this continuous operational envelope is physically impossible, yet uniform random sampling requires an intractable number of runs to uncover rare edge-case failures. Searching beyond isolated trials requires systematically steering disturbances toward the boundary conditions that drive the system closest to its safety limits. Rather than replacing the formal coverage record, automated search routines prioritize which candidate executions deserve empirical evaluation on the physical test track or dynamometer.

15.9.1 Signal temporal logic and quantitative robustness

A binary PASS or FAIL verdict tells an automated search algorithm nothing about whether a passing stop cleared the rack by 15 cm or by 1 mm, leaving the optimizer blind to near misses. Classical temporal logic (Pnueli 1977) checks whether a sequence of states satisfies a property. Signal Temporal Logic (STL) extends the specification to continuous time and real-valued physical signals (such as staying inside a spatial corridor or halting before a deadline (Maler and Nickovic 2004)), and it equips every formula with quantitative robustness semantics (Fainekos and Pappas 2009). Instead of returning a boolean flag, quantitative robustness computes a real-valued signed margin \(\StlRobustness(\StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant) \in \mathbb{R}\) that measures how far a trajectory \(\SignalTrajectory: \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0} \to \mathbb{R}^{\SignalDimension}\) sits from violating or satisfying the temporal property \(\StlFormula\) at time \(\TimeInstant\).

↪ The syntax and semantics of signal temporal logic are developed in Signal Temporal Logic and Continuous Robustness.

For an atomic physical predicate \(\pi \equiv (\StlPredicateFunction(\SignalTrajectory(\TimeInstant)) \ge 0)\) (such as spatial clearance \(\ObstacleSeparationDistance(\TimeInstant) - \SafeSeparationDistance \ge 0\)), quantitative robustness evaluates the signed predicate margin: \(\StlRobustness(\pi, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant) = \StlPredicateFunction(\SignalTrajectory(\TimeInstant))\). Across temporal operators, safety invariants evaluate worst-case (minimum) margins, whereas reachability deadlines evaluate best-case (maximum) margins: \[\StlRobustness(\square_{[0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]} \StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, 0) = \min_{t \in [0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]} \StlRobustness(\StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant), \qquad \StlRobustness(\lozenge_{[0, \StlEventuallyDeadline]} \StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, 0) = \max_{t \in [0, \StlEventuallyDeadline]} \StlRobustness(\StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant). \tag{3}\] Here \(\square_{[0,\FiniteTimeHorizon]}\) denotes “always on \([0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]\)” and \(\lozenge_{[0,\StlEventuallyDeadline]}\) denotes “eventually within \(\StlEventuallyDeadline\).”

The sign and magnitude of \(\StlRobustness\) in equation 3 establish an immediate operational interpretation: \[\StlRobustness > 0 \implies \text{satisfied with positive margin } \StlRobustness, \qquad \StlRobustness < 0 \implies \text{violated with shortfall depth } |\StlRobustness|.\] For example, if the spatial clearance margin is \(\StlRobustness = +0.15\text{ m}\), the minimum clearance observed along the trajectory exceeded the safety threshold by 15 cm. Conversely, if \(\StlRobustness = -0.04\text{ m}\), the machine breached the prescribed clearance boundary by 4 cm, with physical collision depending on whether the safety limit included an explicit standoff buffer.

This signed value margin constitutes space robustness, quantifying how far the signal’s physical magnitude can perturb before the verdict flips. Space robustness captures amplitude margins rather than timing margins: a delayed brake actuation represents a temporal shortfall rather than an instantaneous spatial error. Capturing temporal slack requires time robustness, a complementary semantics that evaluates how far a signal trajectory may shift along the time axis before the verdict changes (Donzé and Maler 2010). Because spatial and temporal margins govern different physical dimensions, combining them into a single unweighted formula introduces semantic ambiguity. For the F1 specification, the cleanest engineering formulation isolates the onset deadline outside the Signal Temporal Logic expression as an explicit scalar predicate on measured reaction time, while evaluating stopping distance via spatial robustness.

Donzé, Alexandre, and Oded Maler. 2010. “Robust Satisfaction of Temporal Logic over Real-Valued Signals.” Formal Modeling and Analysis of Timed Systems (FORMATS), Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 6246: 92–106. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-15297-9_9.

For this reason equation 1 keeps its two predicates apart. The onset predicate, deceleration within 82 ms of the last valid renewal, is a scalar check on measured reaction time, and the travel predicate, at most 751.9 mm at aisle speed, is a space-robustness predicate on vehicle position. Both are measured from the same reference event, so neither can silently absorb slack from the other; a different reference event would change the elapsed timing and require a correspondingly adjusted distance bound.

To illustrate this dual evaluation numerically, consider an F1 trace at aisle speed, timed channel by channel in figure 4, where powered deceleration begins 30 ms late, occurring 112 ms after the last valid renewal. The onset margin against the 82 ms bound evaluates to -30 ms. Because the mobile base travels 39 mm further before braking engages, total travel from renewal to rest reaches 750.9 mm, yielding a distance margin of 1 mm against the 751.9 mm oracle. The onset predicate fails, while nominal travel remains within the distance allowance. The verification results retain both margins in their native physical units; the tracking inset belongs in the oracle and is not additional travel in the plotted speed integral. Because taking an unweighted minimum across milliseconds and millimeters produces an uninterpretable quantity, synthesizing them into a unified score via min requires normalizing each margin by a predeclared positive scale in its corresponding unit. Logging both the raw margins and their normalization factors ensures the synthesized verdict remains physically meaningful. Choosing scales equal to the verification bounds themselves yields normalized margins of -0.366 for onset timing and 0.001 for stopping distance, making the onset predicate the governing term in the combined score. When several predicates have negative margins, alternative scales can change which one governs the compound score, so test engineers must fix normalization factors before executing verification runs. In this nominal trace only onset fails, so positive rescaling cannot make distance the governing predicate. These numerical values reflect model arithmetic over a stipulated reference trace rather than empirical physical measurements.

Recording every monitored channel on one timeline, as illustrated in figure 4, helps localize a failed trial to a stage of the response path when the independent witnesses remain valid. A lease that expires on schedule and a stop command issued at the next tick, followed by a late braking onset, direct the investigation toward command delivery, drive execution, or physical brake response rather than the lease-expiry check.

By producing a continuous, signed margin rather than a binary pass/fail flag, quantitative robustness transforms verification from passive testing into temporal logic falsification as adversarial optimization. In classical computer vision and deep learning, adversarial attacks such as the fast gradient sign method (FGSM) (Goodfellow et al. 2015) and projected gradient descent (PGD) (Madry et al. 2018) search for an input perturbation \(\AdversarialPerturbation\) bounded within an \(\AdversarialPerturbationBudget\)-ball (\(\|\AdversarialPerturbation\|_\infty \le \AdversarialPerturbationBudget\)) that maximizes task loss: \(\max_{\|\AdversarialPerturbation\|_\infty \le \AdversarialPerturbationBudget} \LossFunction(\ParameterizedPredictor(\ModelInput + \AdversarialPerturbation), \ExampleLabel)\). In cyber-physical systems, temporal logic falsification provides the continuous dynamic counterpart. Rather than perturbing static feature vectors, an adversarial optimizer searches over initial state spaces and time-varying physical disturbance trajectories within a bounded physical disturbance envelope to minimize quantitative Signal Temporal Logic robustness. The robustness metric \(\StlRobustness\) acts directly as the adversarial objective function. Whereas uniform random sampling across a high-dimensional operational state space requires an intractable number of physical trials to expose rare corner-case collisions, an adversarial falsification engine actively steers disturbance sequences toward trajectories that drive the system closest to violating its safety invariants.

Goodfellow, I. J., J. Shlens, and C. Szegedy. 2015. “Explaining and Harnessing Adversarial Examples.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Madry, Aleksander, Aleksandar Makelov, Ludwig Schmidt, Dimitris Tsipras, and Adrian Vladu. 2018. “Towards Deep Learning Models Resistant to Adversarial Attacks.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

15.9.2 Formulating falsification as trajectory optimization

An unconstrained mathematical search over arbitrary disturbance signals can easily invent discontinuous spikes that no real actuator, sensor, or contact surface can physically produce, wasting the search on impossible counterexamples. To restrict the search to physically realizable disturbances, let \(\MechanicalPlantState_0 \in \FalsificationInitialStateSet\) represent the machine’s initial state vector (translational speed, payload mass, heading angle, and battery state-of-charge), and let \(\DisturbanceTrajectory(\TimeInstant) \in \AdmissibleDisturbanceSet\) denote an adversarial disturbance trajectory spanning the test horizon \([0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]\). Because physical hardware has finite bandwidth, mechanical inertia, and thermal limits, disturbance trajectories are parameterized over a compact parameter space \(\DisturbanceParameterVector \in \DisturbanceParameterSet\) using smooth basis functions, such as B-splines, cubic splines, or Fourier series: \[\DisturbanceTrajectory(\TimeInstant; \DisturbanceParameterVector) = \sum_{k=1}^{\DisturbanceBasisCount} \DisturbanceBasisCoefficient_k \DisturbanceBasisFunction_k(\TimeInstant)\] where \(\DisturbanceBasisFunction_k(\TimeInstant)\) are time-dependent basis functions and \(\DisturbanceBasisCoefficient_k\) are bounded parameter coefficients.

The temporal logic falsification problem finds the worst-case parameter combination that minimizes quantitative robustness: \[\min_{\MechanicalPlantState_0 \in \FalsificationInitialStateSet, \, \DisturbanceParameterVector \in \DisturbanceParameterSet} \StlRobustness(\StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory(\MechanicalPlantState_0, \DisturbanceTrajectory(\DisturbanceParameterVector)), 0) \quad \text{subject to} \quad \|\DisturbanceTrajectory(\TimeInstant; \DisturbanceParameterVector)\|_\infty \le \DisturbanceBudget, \quad \forall \TimeInstant \in [0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]. \tag{4}\]

The disturbance budget \(\DisturbanceBudget\) in equation 4 bounds the peak magnitude of optical, acoustic, contact, and execution disturbances derived from the operational envelope. Yet magnitude bounds alone do not guarantee that a test bench can deliver the waveform. The basis functions, coefficient bounds, and experimental test fixtures must also respect realistic rates of change and duration limits. For example, a programmable load bank that cannot slew fast enough to replicate a steep simulated voltage transient leaves that synthesized counterexample untested on hardware.

A verification campaign must therefore log both the synthesized disturbance command and its independently measured physical trace. If the test fixture fails to deliver the precommitted disturbance trajectory within specified tolerances, the trial is INVALID for that test condition even if the machine comes to a safe stop. Conversely, while an empirically realized counterexample refutes a safety claim outright, a search that finds no violation within a restricted parameter family leaves arbitrary disturbances outside that basis open to question.

15.9.3 Selecting a search method

Frictional contact transitions and discrete controller state jumps often make the quantitative robustness landscape nonsmooth and non-convex, causing gradient descent to stall or step in the wrong direction unless those transitions are smoothed or handled explicitly. Derivative-free optimizers avoid this trap. The Covariance Matrix Adaptation Evolution Strategy (CMA-ES) (Hansen and Ostermeier 2001) updates a multivariate normal distribution over disturbance parameters across generations, discovering correlated failure modes across speed, surface friction, and execution timing. When physical trials dominate the cost because each run requires mechanical resets, hardware inspection, and thermal cool-down, Bayesian optimization10 fits a probabilistic surrogate model to previous runs to choose the next test point. Neither method guarantees convergence to a global minimum over a finite campaign, so the verification record must preserve search parameter bounds, random seeds, evaluated test vectors, and fixture limits so that auditors can see exactly which regions were challenged.

Hansen, Nikolaus, and Andreas Ostermeier. 2001. “Completely Derandomized Self-Adaptation in Evolution Strategies.” Evolutionary Computation 9 (2): 159–95. https://doi.org/10.1162/106365601750190398.

10 Bayesian Optimization: Originally developed for computationally expensive engineering simulations, Bayesian optimization fits a Gaussian-process surrogate across completed trials and selects the next sample point by maximizing expected improvement (Jones et al. 1998). Physical fault-injection rigs face the same cost structure, because each trial requires mechanical resets, hardware inspection, and thermal stabilization delays. Still, the surrogate’s confidence is only a model prediction: a region it estimates to be safe has not been physically tested simply because the surrogate predicts a positive margin.

Jones, Donald R., Matthias Schonlau, and William J. Welch. 1998. “Efficient Global Optimization of Expensive Black-Box Functions.” Journal of Global Optimization 13 (4): 455–92. https://doi.org/10.1023/A:1008306431147.
Lee, Ritchie, Ole J. Mengshoel, Anshu Saksena, Ryan W. Gardner, Daniel Genin, Joshua Silbermann, Michael Owen, and Mykel J. Kochenderfer. 2020. “Adaptive Stress Testing: Finding Likely Failure Events with Reinforcement Learning.” Journal of Artificial Intelligence Research 69: 1165–201. https://doi.org/10.1613/jair.1.12190.

Two software toolboxes implement this search loop for cyber-physical models: S-TaLiRo (Annpureddy et al. 2011) and Breach (Donzé 2010). Both ingest a formal Signal Temporal Logic requirement and a simulation model, compute quantitative robustness on each simulated rollout, and steer stochastic or local optimizers toward negative robustness. A related family of algorithms treats the disturbance sequence as a sequential decision process. Adaptive stress testing (Lee et al. 2020) explores the disturbance sequence via Monte Carlo tree search or reinforcement learning to find the most probable path to failure rather than an arbitrary, physically improbable edge case, ranking counterexamples by operational plausibility. A survey of these black-box methods groups them by their output (Corso et al. 2021): finding an isolated counterexample, finding the most likely failure trajectory, or estimating an aggregate failure probability. Only the third output measures operational risk, using the estimation procedure developed in section 15.10.2. Whatever search method is chosen, it returns candidate counterexamples and measured robustness margins; when those margins come from a simulator, counting them toward a physical safety claim requires bounding how far the simulator’s trajectories can drift from the real machine’s.

15.9.4 Bounding the sim-to-real transfer gap

A simulator that passes every fault-injection rollout can still hide a physical crash if its modeled trajectories differ from the real machine’s by more than the simulated safety margin. While high-throughput software-in-the-loop simulation is indispensable for exploring combinatorial corner cases, an unvalidated simulator can confirm only what its underlying model assumes. To use simulation sweeps as evidence in a safety case, the engineering team must validate and bound the sim-to-real transfer gap \(\TransferDiscrepancyBound\).

Let \(\SimulatedStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant; \SimulationParameterVector, \ControlInput) \in \TrajectoryStateSpace\) and \(\PhysicalStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant; \SimulationParameterVector, \ControlInput) \in \TrajectoryStateSpace\) denote the system state trajectories in simulation and on target physical hardware under matched declared conditions \(\SimulationParameterVector\) (initial speed, payload mass, floor friction) and control input history \(\ControlInput\). Replaying identical open-loop input sequences establishes a baseline comparison of plant dynamics, but certifying a deployed closed-loop policy requires bounding discrepancy under autonomous execution, where perturbed sensor observations produce divergent actuator commands. The continuous transfer discrepancy is the supremum state error across the test horizon \([0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]\) under a task-weighted physical norm: \[\TransferDiscrepancyBound = \sup_{t \in [0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]} \|\PhysicalStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant) - \SimulatedStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant)\|_{\StateNormWeighting} \tag{5}\] where \(\|\cdot\|_{\StateNormWeighting}\) normalizes position, velocity, and actuator torque errors by predeclared positive reference scales in meters, meters per second, and newton-meters, respectively, before applying dimensionless task weights. The verification plan fixes those scales and weights in advance; the resulting discrepancy \(\TransferDiscrepancyBound\) is dimensionless, while the Lipschitz constant \(\PredicateLipschitzConstant\) carries the physical units of the predicate margin.

A sound safety warrant requires a discrepancy bound that holds across all trajectories in the claimed operational envelope, not just the largest error observed in a small test sample. That modeling error must be deducted directly from the simulated robustness margin. Consider a Signal Temporal Logic safety requirement \(\StlFormula\) whose atomic predicates \(\StlPredicateFunction(\SignalTrajectory(\TimeInstant)) \ge 0\) are Lipschitz continuous with constant \(\PredicateLipschitzConstant = \sup_{\SignalTrajectory_1 \ne \SignalTrajectory_2} |\StlPredicateFunction(\SignalTrajectory_1) - \StlPredicateFunction(\SignalTrajectory_2)| / \|\SignalTrajectory_1 - \SignalTrajectory_2\|_{\StateNormWeighting}\). Here \(\PredicateLipschitzConstant\) is the maximum rate at which the predicate margin degrades per unit of normalized state discrepancy. For a spatial clearance predicate evaluated in meters against position error normalized by a 1 m reference scale with unit task weight, \(\PredicateLipschitzConstant\) is 1 m per unit of normalized discrepancy. By Lipschitz continuity, the physical predicate error is bounded pointwise by the state discrepancy: \[|\StlPredicateFunction(\PhysicalStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant)) - \StlPredicateFunction(\SimulatedStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant))| \le \PredicateLipschitzConstant \|\PhysicalStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant) - \SimulatedStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant)\|_{\StateNormWeighting} \le \PredicateLipschitzConstant \TransferDiscrepancyBound\] which guarantees that at every instant \(\TimeInstant \in [0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]\): \[\StlPredicateFunction(\PhysicalStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant)) \ge \StlPredicateFunction(\SimulatedStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant)) - \PredicateLipschitzConstant \TransferDiscrepancyBound.\] Taking the infimum across the temporal horizon \(\TimeInstant \in [0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]\) for an invariance property \(\StlFormula \equiv \square_{[0, \FiniteTimeHorizon]} (\StlPredicateFunction(\SignalTrajectory(\TimeInstant)) \ge 0)\), the guaranteed lower bound on physical robustness in the real world is: \[\PhysicalStlRobustness \ge \SimulatedStlRobustness - \PredicateLipschitzConstant \TransferDiscrepancyBound. \tag{6}\] The term \(\PredicateLipschitzConstant \TransferDiscrepancyBound\) in equation 6 acts as a sim-to-real robustness discount. If simulated robustness \(\SimulatedStlRobustness\) does not exceed \(\PredicateLipschitzConstant \TransferDiscrepancyBound\), the simulation pass guarantees no physical margin at all: the real machine can breach the safe invariant set (\(\PhysicalStlRobustness < 0\)) even when every simulated rollout returns a positive margin.

The rack-end claim makes this discount concrete. Scoring the distance predicate in meters and normalizing position error by a 1 m reference scale with unit task weight gives a Lipschitz constant of 1 m per unit of normalized discrepancy. Suppose a simulated stop finishes with the entire 131.0 mm reserve intact. The lower bound in equation 6 certifies a positive physical margin when \(\TransferDiscrepancyBound <\) 0.131, meaning the justified position-error bound is smaller than the reserve under this normalization. A larger discrepancy bound leaves the actual physical margin unresolved. A simulator whose stopping position is experimentally validated to within a few centimeters transfers nearly all of its verified margin; a simulator that has never been calibrated against physical stopping runs transfers none of it.

Coupled cyber-physical dynamics determine what this discount must cover: drivetrain compliance and gear backlash alter delivered torque, surface contamination and tire wear change braking traction, and real-time execution jitter shifts braking onset time. A discrepancy bound calibrated on an unloaded, cool machine cannot be silently extended to a loaded vehicle operating under thermal throttling. The verification record must state the operational envelope across which the discrepancy bound holds, keeping that justified bound distinct from the empirical maximum error observed during paired test runs.

Measuring the empirical discrepancy \(\EmpiricalTransferDiscrepancy\) builds on the paired probing protocols used to qualify simulation models for policy training. These protocols drive identical command histories into both simulated and physical hardware and score channel residuals against precommitted tolerances (The sim-to-real contact exploit). They are the same measurements that bound a simulator’s screening validity in Simulation as a conditional proxy. Verification adapts this qualification procedure in two ways. First, excitation inputs are tailored to the specific safety invariant under test, such as full-rate deceleration from aisle speed for the rack-end claim rather than low-speed docking maneuvers. Second, physical trajectories must be captured with external instrumentation independent of the robot’s onboard sensor suite, so that shared sensor calibration errors or estimator drift cannot cancel out of the residual. The resulting empirical discrepancy \(\EmpiricalTransferDiscrepancy\) is archived in the test records alongside the environmental conditions under which it was gathered. If the sim-to-real safety case relies on domain randomization distributions \(\DomainRandomizationDistribution\) to cover hardware variations, it must justify set inclusion across all declared operational conditions: \[\PhysicalTrajectorySet(\SimulationParameterVector) \subseteq \bigcup_{\SampledSimulationParameterVector \sim \DomainRandomizationDistribution} \SimulatedTrajectorySet(\SampledSimulationParameterVector).\] Finite paired measurements can falsify this inclusion by exposing an outlier, but they cannot prove it globally. If unmodeled physical effects such as fieldbus latency jitter, magnetic stator saturation, transmission backlash, or Coulomb stiction cause physical trajectories to escape the randomized simulation bundle, the sim-to-real transfer guarantee fails, and software-in-the-loop simulation passes cannot serve as evidence in the release safety case.

Checkpoint 15.3: Search, robustness, and transfer

A signed margin turns testing into search, and a simulated margin counts toward a physical claim only after its transfer discount.

15.10 Interpreting Campaign Statistics

Counting passed trials in an adversarial campaign can easily mislead a release review into treating a test-bench success rate as an operational reliability number. Any statistical summary describes only the specific population sampled by the test harness, and conditions that were never tested remain missing evidence no matter how sophisticated the statistical model applied afterward.

When \(\ZeroFailureTrialCount\) independent Bernoulli trials sampled from a declared fault distribution yield zero missed mitigations, the outcome puts a one-sided upper confidence limit on the miss probability for that specific distribution. This is the zero-failure trial sizing of Sample size requirements per empirical claim, applied here to an injected hardware fault population rather than closed-loop policy rollouts; at a 95 percent confidence level, it requires about \(3/\TrialFailureProbability\) consecutive successful injections to bound the miss probability below \(\TrialFailureProbability\). To turn these injection counts into a hardware coverage claim weighted by each component’s failure rate in FIT (failures in time, one per \(10^9\) operating hours; notebook 15.3 gives the component weights \(\ComponentFailureRate_i\)), the injection harness must sample faults in direct proportion to their raw failure rates \(\ComponentFailureRate_i\). Otherwise, the verification pipeline must apply an explicitly justified stratified weighting across each fault stratum.11

11 Trial sampling and aging: The binomial count assumes independent, identically distributed Bernoulli outcomes under the declared injection distribution; it does not require Poisson event arrivals. Repeated heating, mechanical wear, or contact changes can break independence and require a state-stratified experimental design.

This statistical bound holds only when the physical trials satisfy the declared independence and sampling assumptions. On real hardware, repeated high-energy stops produce Joule heating (\(\WindingCurrent^2\ElectricalResistance\)) in the drive motor windings and mechanical brake linings. That heat buildup changes winding resistance and lining friction coefficients, coupling successive trials. As a result, a test campaign run on a dry aisle at room temperature cannot support a confidence bound for a contaminated floor or for the operating envelope’s 5 °C thermal extreme.

Notebook 15.3 draws its rates from four component strata (sensors, compute, transceivers and power management, gate drivers), and each stratum maps onto rows of table 2. Sensor faults are injected as F2 and as the physical transducer disturbances, compute faults as F1, transceiver and power-management faults as F3 and F4, and gate-driver faults as the drive that keeps producing torque after a stop command (section 15.6.2). Sampling in proportion to those rates therefore fixes how the campaign’s trials divide among the rows.

Napkin Math 15.3: The coverage count
Problem: Illustrative component fault rates and a chosen ceiling on the unmitigated rate fix the diagnostic coverage the machine needs. How many zero-miss injections, sampled in proportion to those rates, would demonstrate that coverage?

Variables:

  • Component fault rates \(\ComponentFailureRate_i\) in FIT, one fault per \(10^9\) operating hours (Illustrative): sensors 1,500, compute 1,000, transceivers and power management 2,500, and gate drivers 4,800.
  • Target unmitigated rate \(\TargetHazardRate =\) 100 FIT, with a stricter 10 FIT alternative, at confidence \(\ConfidenceLevel =\) 95 percent (Chosen).
