  • Diagnostic coverage \(\DiagnosticCoverage\), the \(\ComponentFailureRate_i\)-weighted fraction of faults whose hazardous effect is mitigated, so that \(\UnmitigatedHardwareFaultRate = \RawHardwareFaultRate(1 - \DiagnosticCoverage)\) and a single injection misses with probability \(\TrialFailureProbability = 1 - \DiagnosticCoverage\).

Math:

Required coverage. \[\lambda_{\text{raw}} = 1500 + 1000 + 2500 + 4800 = 9800\ \text{FIT}, \qquad C_{\text{diag}} \ge 1 - \frac{\lambda_{\text{target}}}{\lambda_{\text{raw}}} = 1 - \frac{100}{9800} = 0.9898\]

Injections to demonstrate it, from the zero-failure count of Sample size requirements per empirical claim. \[n \ge \frac{\ln(1 - C)}{\ln C_{\text{diag}}} = \frac{\ln 0.05}{\ln 0.9898} = 292.1 \;\Rightarrow\; n = 293, \qquad \frac{3}{p} = \frac{3 \lambda_{\text{raw}}}{\lambda_{\text{target}}} = \frac{3 \times 9800}{100} = 294\]

Sensitivity. The count scales as \(3\RawHardwareFaultRate/\TargetHazardRate\), so the 10 FIT target needs 99.90 percent coverage and 2,935 injections, against a rule-of-three estimate of 2,940.

Result: A 100 FIT target needs 98.98 percent coverage, which 293 zero-miss injections demonstrate at 95 percent confidence.

Systems insight: The target unmitigated failure rate, not the number of entries in the fault list, governs campaign size. Every tenfold tightening of the target rate demands roughly a tenfold increase in physical injection runs. Furthermore, this sample size holds only when injections are drawn in proportion to each component’s raw failure rate \(\ComponentFailureRate_i\). Injecting uniformly across components with disparate failure rates tests a distorted distribution unless the harness weights the empirical outcomes and gathers enough trials within every stratum. Every trial must therefore log the exact injected mode, whether hardware detection triggered within the deadline, and whether the safety controller brought the plant to a verified safe state.

Even within the declared fault strata, a campaign rarely reaches every combination of load, floor friction, and temperature. The fault-record set must therefore catalog omitted test cases as carefully as completed runs, recording every parameter region skipped because of testbed physical limits, sensor bandwidth limits, or destructive testing costs. Unprobed regions cannot count as verified safety margins in the release case, nor can passive operational hours accumulated outside the test cell fill those gaps.

15.10.1 Exposure and the population a statistic describes

Logging thousands of incident-free fleet hours in normal warehouse aisles cannot bound rare safety-critical faults or substitute for targeted fault injection, because passive operation samples a different distribution from deliberate fault trials. Fleet exposure runs into the exposure wall of equation 7.9: bounding a hazard rate \(\TargetHazardRate\) at 95 percent confidence requires about \(3/\TargetHazardRate\) failure-free operating hours, which Butler and Finelli (1993) showed is unreachable at safety-critical rates such as \(10^{-9}\) failures per hour. Pooling those hours across a fleet adds exposure time but not environmental diversity, so the bound covers only the conditions the pooled machines share (Verification Ceilings and the Exposure Wall), and normal fleet traffic rarely triggers the corner-case hardware faults this chapter injects. Verification also has to ask which configuration those hours describe. Any change to a safety filter, control policy, or actuator driver can alter the sampled outcomes, so earlier fleet hours count toward the updated configuration only when an explicit engineering argument shows that the change left the counted events unaffected. When a rare failure rate must be estimated across a high-dimensional disturbance space without waiting for billions of passive rollouts, the campaign must steer samples toward the failure region and weight them back to the operational distribution.

Butler, Ricky W., and George B. Finelli. 1993. “The Infeasibility of Quantifying the Reliability of Life-Critical Real-Time Software.” IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering 19 (1): 3–12. https://doi.org/10.1109/32.210303.

15.10.2 Estimating a rare failure rate by importance sampling

Naive Monte Carlo sampling from nominal operating conditions cannot estimate rare safety-critical failure rates because nearly every rollout ends in an unremarkable, safe stop, requiring billions of runs to observe even a handful of violations (section 15.10.1). While adversarial falsification finds whether an unsafe trajectory exists, release decisions also need to know how often such failures occur under nominal operating conditions. Importance sampling12 breaks this bottleneck by drawing rollouts from an alternative proposal distribution that concentrates samples near the failure boundary and then weighting each sample by its likelihood ratio to recover an unbiased estimate under the operational distribution.

12 Importance Sampling: The technique originated in Monte Carlo calculations of nuclear radiation shielding in the early 1950s, where the few particles that penetrated dense shielding were exceptionally rare yet critical to safety. Kahn and Marshall (1953) formalized importance sampling alongside correlated sampling and statistical estimation to cut required sample counts. The method carries a sharp failure mode of its own: a proposal distribution that omits a valid failure path reports tiny sample variance and an erroneously low failure probability, a support failure that diagnostic checks must catch.

Kahn, Herman, and Andrew W. Marshall. 1953. “Methods of Reducing Sample Size in Monte Carlo Computations.” Journal of the Operations Research Society of America 1 (5): 263–78. https://doi.org/10.1287/opre.1.5.263.

Let the operational distribution of uncertain input parameters \(\UncertainScenarioParameters\) be denoted by \(\OperationalScenarioDistribution(\UncertainScenarioParameters)\), and let \(\FailureRegion\) define the subset of conditions leading to a safety violation. Standard Monte Carlo estimation evaluates the failure probability \(\OperationalFailureProbability = \Pr_{\OperationalScenarioDistribution}(\UncertainScenarioParameters \in \FailureRegion)\) by computing the empirical failure fraction across \(\EstimatorDrawCount\) independent draws from \(\OperationalScenarioDistribution\). Importance sampling instead draws samples from an engineered proposal distribution \(\ImportanceProposalDistribution(\UncertainScenarioParameters)\) and weights each outcome by the likelihood ratio \(\LikelihoodRatioWeight(\UncertainScenarioParameters) = \OperationalScenarioDistribution(\UncertainScenarioParameters)/\ImportanceProposalDistribution(\UncertainScenarioParameters)\): \[\ImportanceSampledFailureProbability = \frac{1}{\EstimatorDrawCount}\sum_{i=1}^{\EstimatorDrawCount} \mathbb{1}[\UncertainScenarioParameters_i \in \FailureRegion]\, \LikelihoodRatioWeight(\UncertainScenarioParameters_i), \qquad \UncertainScenarioParameters_i \sim \ImportanceProposalDistribution. \tag{7}\] This estimator remains mathematically unbiased as long as \(\ImportanceProposalDistribution(\UncertainScenarioParameters) > 0\) whenever \(\OperationalScenarioDistribution(\UncertainScenarioParameters)\mathbb{1}[\UncertainScenarioParameters \in \FailureRegion] > 0\), since the likelihood weight corrects for the shift in sampling probability (Owen 2013). The variance of \(\ImportanceSampledFailureProbability\) depends entirely on the geometry of \(\ImportanceProposalDistribution\). The theoretically optimal zero-variance proposal is the operational distribution \(\OperationalScenarioDistribution\) conditioned on the failure region \(\FailureRegion\) (\(\ImportanceProposalDistribution^*(\UncertainScenarioParameters) = \OperationalScenarioDistribution(\UncertainScenarioParameters)\mathbb{1}[\UncertainScenarioParameters \in \FailureRegion] / \OperationalFailureProbability\)). Constructing \(\ImportanceProposalDistribution^*\) directly is impossible because its normalizing constant is the very quantity \(\OperationalFailureProbability\) under estimation. Practical verification pipelines must therefore construct parametric approximations to \(\ImportanceProposalDistribution^*\).

The cross-entropy method (Rubinstein and Kroese 2004) systematically discovers such an approximation within a parameterized distribution family \(\ParametricProposalDistribution\) by solving a sequence of intermediate rare-event problems. Beginning with an initial proposal aligned with the operational profile (\(\InitialProposalDistribution = \OperationalScenarioDistribution\)), the algorithm iteratively draws a sample batch from the current proposal and evaluates the performance metric. It then selects an intermediate threshold at the \((1-\EliteSampleFraction)\) quantile so that a fraction \(\EliteSampleFraction\) of the batch qualifies as elite runs. The parameter vector \(\ProposalParameterVector\) is then updated by maximizing the likelihood-weighted log-density of these elite samples, which minimizes the Kullback-Leibler divergence (a measure of how far one probability distribution lies from another) to the optimal proposal on that intermediate slice. As the threshold steps forward across iterations, the proposal gradually shifts its probability mass toward the true failure boundary. For an exponential proposal parameterized by its mean, or a fixed-variance Gaussian proposal parameterized by its mean, this update admits an exact closed form: the updated mean is the weighted empirical mean of the elite draws.

Consider this method applied to the rack-end deceleration scenario. Traveling at nominal aisle velocity, the mobile base consumes its 131.0 mm stopping reserve from section 15.2 in \(\FallbackDeadline =\) 100.8 ms, the latest-fallback deadline of equation 14.4 at aisle speed. Any unbudgeted message queue delay exceeding \(\FallbackDeadline\) exhausts this safety buffer, causing the physical vehicle to breach the clearance boundary. To establish an analytical baseline, assume an illustrative operational distribution where queue delays \(\MessageQueueDelay\) follow an exponential distribution with mean \(\MeanMessageQueueDelay =\) 5 ms (in production verification, latency measurements gathered under the declared load must justify or replace this model). Under this formulation, the analytical failure probability is \(\OperationalFailureProbability = e^{-\FallbackDeadline/\MeanMessageQueueDelay} =\) 1.8 × 10⁻⁹ per stopping event. This closed-form quantity serves as an exact ground truth against which to benchmark competing sampling estimators. The threshold is deliberately the uncharged ceiling of section 15.2, which makes the example optimistic. Charging the dispatch-to-admission allocation leaves only 37.5 ms, and the same delay model then gives \(\OperationalFailureProbability =\) 5.6 × 10⁻⁴, about 3 × 10⁵ times larger. The comparison below keeps the uncharged threshold because there the failure is rarest and the gap between estimators widest; at the charged threshold even plain Monte Carlo would need far fewer runs.

The sample sizing required across these estimators stems directly from the variance of sample averages. When an estimator averages \(\EstimatorDrawCount\) independent draws with mean \(\OperationalFailureProbability\) and per-sample variance \(\EstimatorDrawStandardDeviation^2\), the estimator’s standard deviation is \(\EstimatorDrawStandardDeviation/\sqrt{\EstimatorDrawCount}\), yielding a relative standard error \(\RelativeStandardError = \sqrt{\EstimatorDrawStandardDeviation^2 / (\OperationalFailureProbability^2 \EstimatorDrawCount)}\). Achieving a target relative precision \(\RelativeStandardError\) requires \(\EstimatorDrawCount = (\EstimatorDrawStandardDeviation^2 / \OperationalFailureProbability^2) / \RelativeStandardError^2\) trials, where \(\EstimatorDrawStandardDeviation^2 / \OperationalFailureProbability^2\) denotes the per-draw relative variance. For naive Monte Carlo, each trial is an indicator variable with variance \(\OperationalFailureProbability(1 - \OperationalFailureProbability)\), demanding approximately \((1 - \OperationalFailureProbability)/(\OperationalFailureProbability \RelativeStandardError^2)\) simulated runs. Under the illustrative delay model, securing an estimate with \(\RelativeStandardError =\) 10 percent relative error requires 5.7 × 10¹⁰ trials. By comparison, a zero-failure qualification campaign requires 1.7 × 10⁹ consecutive incident-free runs to establish an upper bound at the \(\OperationalFailureProbability\) threshold (equation 7.4). Under importance sampling for this exponential model, the per-draw variance equals the weighted failure indicator’s second moment minus \(\OperationalFailureProbability^2\), which stays finite as long as the proposal rate is less than twice the operational rate.

The cross-entropy method using exponential proposals with an elite fraction of \(\EliteSampleFraction =\) 10 percent demonstrates how adaptive sampling overcomes this barrier. The initial threshold is set at the 90th percentile of the operational delay distribution (\(\MeanMessageQueueDelay\ln 10\), or 11.5 ms). Because the exponential distribution is memoryless, the conditional distribution of delays exceeding any threshold \(\CrossEntropyLevelThreshold\) is identical to the original distribution shifted by \(\CrossEntropyLevelThreshold\). The likelihood-weighted mean of the elite samples is therefore simply \(\CrossEntropyLevelThreshold + \MeanMessageQueueDelay\), which immediately defines the mean for the next proposal. Because the parametric proposal family consists of unshifted exponential distributions supported on the positive real line rather than truncated distributions starting at \(\CrossEntropyLevelThreshold\), matching this conditional mean stretches the exponential scale across the entire positive axis rather than shifting the lower support boundary. Successive iterations lift the intermediate threshold to 38.0 ms and 99.1 ms. By iteration 4, the intermediate quantile surpasses the critical threshold, clamping the final level at \(\FallbackDeadline\) with a proposal mean of 105.8 ms, the conditional expectation of the delay given a boundary violation. Under this adapted proposal, 38.6 percent of the generated rollouts land in the failure set, and their small likelihood weights scale each observed failure back to the operational probability. The resulting per-draw relative variance drops to 27.1. Consequently, only 2,709 simulation runs are needed to reach the target 10 percent relative error, a factor of 2 × 10⁷ fewer runs than plain Monte Carlo and within 0.1 percent of the best exponential proposal. These numerical counts derive from the assumed exponential model; testing against an empirical delay histogram will yield distinct run requirements.

Weighting runs near failure cuts the runs by about 2 × 10⁷ times.

Two statistical diagnostics safeguard the integrity of importance sampling estimates. The first is the effective sample size, defined as \(\EffectiveSampleSize = \left(\sum_{i=1}^{\EstimatorDrawCount} \LikelihoodRatioWeight_i\right)^2 / \sum_{i=1}^{\EstimatorDrawCount} \LikelihoodRatioWeight_i^2\) (Owen 2013). This metric diagnoses concentration of the likelihood weights; it does not by itself establish the precision of the weighted failure estimate, which depends on the variance of the weighted failure indicator. For the 2,709 runs above, the expected effective sample size is approximately 250; a degenerate value approaching 1 would warn that a few outlying weights dominate the summation, rendering the estimate unstable. The second diagnostic is distribution support and tail coverage. Omitting a subregion of \(\FailureRegion\) with positive operational probability violates the support condition and biases the estimate. A proposal that retains support but samples the operational tail too rarely can instead produce unstable or infinite variance, with deceptively small observed variance in a finite run. Sample variance cannot reveal an omitted failure path that generates no samples, so the campaign must justify coverage independently of its weight diagnostics. O’Kelly et al. (2018) applied adaptive importance sampling to end-to-end verification of autonomous driving software stacks and obtained speedups of several orders of magnitude over naive Monte Carlo in estimating rare collision rates.

This structure separates importance sampling from adversarial falsification. Although an adversarial search engine also concentrates runs near the system’s safety boundaries, its sampling density is unknown. Because falsification runs lack likelihood-ratio weights, the fraction of falsification runs that fail says nothing about real-world failure frequency; in the illustrative example, 38.6 percent of the proposal runs fail even though the modeled operational failure probability is only 1.8 × 10⁻⁹. A quantitative failure-probability estimate belongs in a safety case only when paired with its declared operational model \(\OperationalScenarioDistribution\), proposal distribution \(\ImportanceProposalDistribution\), weight history, effective sample size, and confidence intervals. It remains a statement about that declared operating model rather than a direct physical measurement of the deployed hardware. Estimating how often a boundary is crossed still leaves a second question open: when a near-miss or excursion occurs, how far can the worst-case physical overshoot reach?

15.10.3 Extreme value theory and heavy-tailed safety distributions

Binary pass-fail tallies throw away the margin history of near-miss stops, while fitting a Gaussian curve to nominal bus traffic or stopping-distance data underestimates the heavy tails caused by sporadic bus arbitration contention or contact slips. Deployment qualification often has to bound the physical magnitude of worst-case excursions across continuous telemetry streams: spatial clearance \(\ClearDistance - \BaseTravelDistance(\TimeInstant)\), onset slack \(\HazardOnsetBound - \HazardOnset\), and actuator torque headroom \(\JointTorqueLimit - \DeliveredActuatorTorque\). Extreme value theory (EVT) provides limiting distributions for these tail excursions, playing a role for extreme deviations similar to that of the central limit theorem for sample means (Coles 2001). Even so, engineers must check that the data belong to the assumed tail regime rather than treating a fitted mathematical endpoint as a physical barrier. While the block-maxima formulation (which yields the Generalized Extreme Value distribution) is developed in Extreme Value Theory and Tail Risk, cyber-physical verification primarily uses the threshold-exceedance formulation, which keeps every observation that crosses a high safety threshold rather than discarding all but the block maximum.

Coles, Stuart. 2001. An Introduction to Statistical Modeling of Extreme Values. Springer.

  1. Peaks over threshold and the Generalized Pareto Distribution: Let \(\TailPerformanceMetric\) denote a continuous performance metric (such as stopping-distance error), and let \(\ExceedanceThreshold\) represent a high baseline threshold. We model the exceedances \(\ThresholdExcessVariable = \TailPerformanceMetric - \ExceedanceThreshold\) conditional on \(\TailPerformanceMetric > \ExceedanceThreshold\). For distributions in an appropriate extreme-value domain of attraction, the Pickands-Balkema-de Haan theorem gives an asymptotic Generalized Pareto approximation to the conditional excess distribution as \(\ExceedanceThreshold\) approaches the upper endpoint. Balkema and Haan (1974) and Pickands (1975) proved this result independently: \[\GpdCumulativeDistribution(\EvaluatedExceedance; \GpdScaleParameter, \TailShapeParameter) = 1 - \left( 1 + \frac{\TailShapeParameter \EvaluatedExceedance}{\GpdScaleParameter} \right)^{-1/\TailShapeParameter}, \quad \EvaluatedExceedance > 0, \tag{8}\] defined for scale parameter \(\GpdScaleParameter > 0\) on the support \(1 + \TailShapeParameter \EvaluatedExceedance/\GpdScaleParameter > 0\), with the exponential distribution recovered as the continuous limit \(\TailShapeParameter \to 0\). The shape parameter \(\TailShapeParameter\) governs tail thickness: \(\TailShapeParameter > 0\) indicates a heavy, power-law tail; \(\TailShapeParameter = 0\) corresponds to an exponential tail; and \(\TailShapeParameter < 0\) defines a bounded tail with finite endpoint \(\ExceedanceThreshold - \GpdScaleParameter/\TailShapeParameter\).

  2. Return level: For a chosen return period13 \(\ReturnPeriodTrialCount\) calibrated to the sampling interval, the return level \(\ReturnLevel_{\ReturnPeriodTrialCount}\) is the threshold exceeded on average once every \(\ReturnPeriodTrialCount\) periods: \[\ReturnLevel_{\ReturnPeriodTrialCount} = \ExceedanceThreshold + \frac{\GpdScaleParameter}{\TailShapeParameter} \left[ (\ReturnPeriodTrialCount \ThresholdExceedanceRate)^{\TailShapeParameter} - 1 \right] \tag{9}\] where \(\ThresholdExceedanceRate = \Pr(\TailPerformanceMetric > \ExceedanceThreshold) = \ThresholdExceedanceCount / \TotalExecutedTrialCount\) is the empirical exceedance rate across \(\TotalExecutedTrialCount\) trials, with \(\ReturnPeriodTrialCount\) expressed in equivalent trial counts. This expression follows from multiplying the conditional Generalized Pareto survival probability \(\left(1 + \TailShapeParameter(\ReturnLevel_{\ReturnPeriodTrialCount} - \ExceedanceThreshold)/\GpdScaleParameter\right)^{-1/\TailShapeParameter}\) by the threshold exceedance probability \(\ThresholdExceedanceRate\) and setting the resulting unconditional tail probability equal to \(1/\ReturnPeriodTrialCount\). In this subsection \(\ReturnPeriodTrialCount\) is therefore a count of trials, not the test horizon of equation 4. Equation 9 holds for \(\TailShapeParameter \ne 0\); its continuous limit at \(\TailShapeParameter = 0\) is \(\ReturnLevel_{\ReturnPeriodTrialCount} = \ExceedanceThreshold + \GpdScaleParameter\ln(\ReturnPeriodTrialCount\ThresholdExceedanceRate)\). Expressing a return period in calendar operating hours requires scaling by an independently justified hourly event arrival rate under a verified temporal dependence model. The product \(\ReturnPeriodTrialCount\ThresholdExceedanceRate\) is dimensionless, and valid extrapolations must evaluate return levels at or above the conditioning threshold \(\ExceedanceThreshold\).

13 Return Period: The term comes from civil hydrology, where a “hundred-year flood” means a discharge level with a 0.01 probability of being exceeded in any single year (Gumbel 1958). The hydrological caveat applies directly to robotics: because return levels extrapolate beyond observed records, they hold only when the threshold choice and sampling distribution are physically justified. Counting return periods across synthetic fault-injection runs gives no operational safety assurance until the hourly arrival rate in service is established.

Gumbel, Emil J. 1958. Statistics of Extremes. Columbia University Press.

Connecting adversarial falsification to EVT tail sampling

Under nominal operation, exceedances past a conservative margin threshold occur far too rarely to fit an empirical tail; the exposure wall of section 15.10.1 starves extreme-value fits just as severely as binary failure counts. Adversarial falsification gets around this scarcity by concentrating rollouts near violation boundaries, solving the search problem of equation 4 over initial plant states and disturbance parameters. Let \(\KnownComponentStopDistance\) denote the design budget at the trial speed, 969.0 mm at aisle speed, so that \(\ClearDistance - \KnownComponentStopDistance\) is the 131.0 mm spare. For the braking-distance overshoot metric \(\TailPerformanceMetric = \StoppingDistance - \KnownComponentStopDistance\), the search populates the exceedance set \[\ExceedanceSampleSet = \{ \ThresholdExcessVariable_i = \TailPerformanceMetric_i - \ExceedanceThreshold \mid \TailPerformanceMetric_i > \ExceedanceThreshold \}\] with dense observations near the failure frontier. Those samples, however, reflect the adversary’s search policy rather than the operational environment. Fitting a Generalized Pareto Distribution directly to unweighted falsification runs models the optimizer’s search behavior, not the reliability of the physical plant. To inform operational risk, the runs must come from a declared proposal distribution and carry the likelihood-ratio weights of equation 7, so that both the exceedance probability \(\ThresholdExceedanceRate\) and the fitted parameters reflect weighted operational likelihoods.

A fitted shape parameter serves as an engineering diagnostic rather than a standalone safety verdict:

  • Positive shape (\(\TailShapeParameter > 0\)): The fitted model extrapolates a heavy upper tail, warning of sensitivity to extreme deviations. This fit does not prove that physical overshoots are truly unbounded, but it requires the engineering team to check whether threshold choice, sensor noise, or unmodeled delays drive the heavy-tailed behavior.
  • Negative shape (\(\TailShapeParameter < 0\)): The fitted distribution implies a finite modeled upper endpoint at \(\ExceedanceThreshold - \GpdScaleParameter/\TailShapeParameter\). That mathematical endpoint must never be mistaken for a certified hardware stop or taken as proof that a runtime safety filter will always contain motion. Verifying real-world containment requires auditing the filter’s dynamical assumptions and measuring its hardware response latencies under adversarial load.

Sim-to-real transfer bounds on EVT return levels

When high-throughput campaigns run on the software-in-the-loop and processor-in-the-loop (PIL) rungs of the qualification ladder, extreme value modeling produces a simulated return level \(\SimulatedReturnLevel\). As shown in section 15.9.4, physical trajectories can diverge from simulated trajectories by up to the sim-to-real discrepancy bound \(\TransferDiscrepancyBound = \sup_t \|\PhysicalStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant) - \SimulatedStateTrajectory(\TimeInstant)\|_{\StateNormWeighting}\). When simulated and physical trials describe the same operational population and the overshoot metric obeys the pathwise discrepancy bound with constant \(\PredicateLipschitzConstant\), that bound also limits the physical return level (equation 10): \[\PhysicalReturnLevel \le \SimulatedReturnLevel + \PredicateLipschitzConstant \TransferDiscrepancyBound. \tag{10}\] To support a stopping-distance claim, this widened return level must fit inside the available physical reserve: \[\SimulatedReturnLevel + \PredicateLipschitzConstant \TransferDiscrepancyBound \le \ClearDistance - \KnownComponentStopDistance. \tag{11}\]

The margin inequality in equation 11 is a necessary sanity check rather than a sufficient release rule. Using it requires distributional consistency between simulation and physical testbeds, proper importance weighting when quoting operational return periods, confidence intervals on the fitted parameters, and a transfer bound \(\TransferDiscrepancyBound\) justified across the entire operating domain. A small trajectory discrepancy across a handful of bench tests does not establish an envelope-wide sim-to-real bound.

In the rack-end deceleration scenario, the physical safety margin is 131.0 mm, and only 48.7 mm once the dispatch-to-admission allocation is charged, so a return level of a few centimeters, widened by a transfer bound of a few centimeters more, already consumes it. Any proposed tail bound must state its sample population, proposal weights, parameter uncertainties, and transfer error bounds before being compared against this reserve. When a fault mode (such as mode F1) has zero measured physical trials, it supports neither an empirical tail fit nor a return-level estimate; a fitted mathematical endpoint cannot stand in for missing physical test evidence.

Every statistical metric in this section is conditional. A zero-miss count describes only the declared fault injection distribution. An exposure bound reflects only the exact hardware and software configuration that accumulated those hours. An importance-sampling estimate holds only under its declared operational model. An extreme value return level holds only relative to its fitted tail and sim-to-real discrepancy bound. Because any of these premises can fail, a verification campaign never hands release an isolated scalar margin; instead, it packages fault records that bind each empirical margin to the physical, environmental, and statistical conditions that support it.

15.11 Fault Records for Release

A raw telemetry trace sitting on a testbench digitizer cannot certify an autonomous machine for release if nothing proves which safety claim, envelope corner, or software and hardware build produced it. In an adversarial campaign, a completed F1 trial records the full event chain across independent physical channels: the final proposal renewal, the watchdog lease expiration, the active braking response, and the final mechanical stop. The release authority can credit that trial only when the measured trace is verifiably bound to the safety claim, the tested envelope point, and the exact software and hardware builds under evaluation. The fault record establishes this link by binding the structured test header to the retained evidence bundle through a cryptographic digest.

The release authority receives these claim-specific fault records together with the coverage records needed to judge their physical scope. Combined, they form an auditable fault-record set that joins the individual fault records, qualification environments, justified sim-to-real transfer bounds, statistical tail estimates, and known coverage gaps. Rather than silently omitting unmeasured or inapplicable fields, the schema marks them explicitly so that untested operating regimes cannot pass for verified boundaries. This fault-record set feeds directly into the operational safety case for release, though its schema and cryptographic signatures carry no authority beyond the physical evidence recorded inside them.

Along with design contracts, the motion-authority cases A1–A4, and the placement allocations established during system staging, the fault records draw on two upstream records: the omission log of the policy manifest (The Policy Manifest) and the coverage-gap catalog of the evaluation record (The Evaluation Record). Those upstream records list the operating conditions, sensor degradation modes, and environmental disturbances that the learned policy proposer never saw during training. By pairing the record header with its evidence bundle, each fault record preserves the full provenance of the trial: the asserted claim, physical operating point, fault injection mechanism, hardware and software revisions, raw high-speed telemetry, detection mechanisms, response latencies, physical stopping outcomes, and any secondary anomalies observed during the run.

15.11.1 The fault-record set specification

A pile of individual pass verdicts leaves a release review unable to tell which top-level safety claims have been substantiated across the envelope and which operating conditions remain untested. Every record in the fault-record set therefore exists to support one named assurance claim \(\AssuranceClaim\) within the release safety case, and its header names that claim explicitly. In the safety case structure developed in Claims, arguments, and warrants, the top-level assurance claim \(\AssuranceClaim_0\) states a core physical invariant, such as halting within the clearance corridor (\(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\)), and splits into sub-claims \(\AssuranceClaim_i\) covering specific operating envelopes, hardware fault classes, and fallback behaviors. The F1, F2, and F3 records support that stopping bound across communication and timing faults, showing that the safety permission path halts the loaded mobile base within its travel bound once proposal renewals cease (F1), when sensor observations arrive stale (F2), or when fieldbus frames drop in transit (F3). Where F1–F3 test information and timing loss, the F4 and actuator-jam records test electrical and mechanical survival. F4 shows that the held-up auxiliary power rail keeps the permission path alive through a primary supply collapse long enough to complete an emergency stop and set all mechanical holding brakes. The actuator-jam records show that the chassis stays within its lateral clearance corridor during a wheel-lock fault without driving the stator windings into thermal runaway. At the control boundary, the A1–A4 records support the claims that motion authority keeps a single writer, blocks control bypass, runs a deterministic fallback when supervisory handovers stall, and rejects forged command tokens. Alongside them, the proposer-injection records show whether tested adversarial proposals were caught by the runtime permission checks. Extending that result to every possible proposal still requires an architectural or formal argument that no source can bypass the permission path.

A physical trace supports an assurance claim \(\AssuranceClaim\) only through a structured engineering argument. In a rack-end deceleration claim, for instance, a raw brake-onset trace supplies empirical evidence, but the accompanying argument must explain why the digitizer clock synchronization, fault-injection timing, payload distribution, and floor traction conditions test the claimed physical response. Gathering evidence across the qualification ladder (formal reachability bounds for the permission filter, bounded sim-to-real falsification campaigns, and hardware fault-injection trials on the rig) lets each assurance claim \(\AssuranceClaim\) rest on complementary legs of evidence rather than a single isolated trial. How claims, arguments, and evidence fit together into an auditable assurance case, and the notation used to write that hierarchy down, are developed in Claims, arguments, and warrants.

The fault records also separate three failure domains, because each asks a different engineering question about the same physical machine, even when a compound fault spans more than one domain:

  • Functional safety: hardware and software component faults inside the safety-related control system, such as encoder failures, dropped fieldbus frames, or auxiliary power brownouts.
  • Safety of the intended functionality (SOTIF): hazards that arise without hardware faults from algorithmic blind spots or sensor limits, such as perceptual occlusions, out-of-distribution optical flare, or corner-case proposer errors.
  • Security: deliberate adversarial tampering with physical sensors, communication buses, or cryptographic tokens.

The consensus engineering standards covering each subsystem of the machine, along with their publication editions, are mapped in The standards map for the running machine. Passing an adversarial fault-injection campaign does not by itself establish regulatory compliance or justify operation in unmodeled environments; instead, the release argument weighs exactly which claims the assembled records substantiate and where residual operational risks remain. To support that review, the individual fault records, the per-rung coverage of the qualification ladder, the empirical sim-to-real discrepancy bounds, and the extreme value theory (EVT) tail parameters assemble into one cryptographically signed set. This fault-record set is the thirteenth record in the record chain, after the authority record and before the release manifest, and it carries verification evidence to the release gate.

↪ The record chain, numbered from the limit record to the residual-claims register, is developed in The record register.

The signed fault-record set defines the evidentiary handoff between adversarial testing and operational release, with its full data schema specified in table 5:

  1. Upstream Custody Lineage:
    • Consumes the authority record (The Authority Record) to check handover deadline timers (\(\HandoverTimeout\), Clearance-derived fallback deadlines), duplicate-writer rejections (A1), and supervisory token integrity.
    • Consumes the placement record (The Placement Record) to check bus contention limits, power-rail droop tolerances, and thermal dissipation thresholds.
    • Consumes the enforcement record and its admitted commands (The Enforcement Record) to check control barrier function (CBF) invariant sets, quadratic program (QP) filter execution deadlines, and fallback ladder transitions.
    • Consumes the evaluation record (The Evaluation Record) to cross-check offline covariate drift and simulation test catalogs against physical target-hardware runs.
  2. Downstream Release Deliverables:
    • Delivers to the release manifest and its safety case (Safety Cases and Claims) the empirical, claim-specific evidence and operational bounds needed by the release argument.
    • Delivers to the residual-claims register (A residual-claims register) the explicit list of unverified operating regimes, test-fixture bandwidth limits, and coverage gaps.

Capturing these eight fields in a signed record set prevents post-hoc threshold tuning and silent omission of failed or unobservable trials. Even a complete, cryptographically signed fault-record set, however, can be undermined if hidden electrical, bus, or clock couplings on the test bench corrupt the trials or blind the witnesses that score them. Four recurring pitfalls illustrate how shared resources across watchdogs, fieldbuses, power rails, and loggers can subvert a verification campaign before a single record reaches release.

15.12 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A physical machine can pass every isolated component fault test yet catastrophically fail in deployment when a shared power rail, communication bus, or reference clock couples multiple subsystems or blinds their diagnostic monitors. These failures stem from unintended causal, electrical, and temporal coupling across architectural boundaries. Compound verification tests catch such cross-domain hazards only when enough galvanically and logically independent instrumentation survives the fault to establish the ground-truth sequence of events.

Fallacy: Independent local watchdog timers guarantee end-to-end physical deadline compliance.

A perception front-end, the chunk policy, and the safety permission monitor can each execute comfortably within their local scheduling slices while an unmonitored inter-process queue buffers stale observations between them. Local watchdog timers confirm only that isolated tasks kick their respective heartbeats within software deadlines; they reveal nothing about the total age of the physical state traversing the distributed pipeline.

Consider an asynchronous buffer that inserts delay into the machine’s 133.6 ms pre-deceleration budget. The importance-sampling example of section 15.10.2 already priced this queue. At 1.3 m/s it exhausts the spare after 100.8 ms, only 75.4 percent of the nominal pre-deceleration interval, and once the dispatch-to-admission allocation is charged it does so after 37.5 ms. The fallacy lies in what the watchdogs report while it happens. Every task-level watchdog shows nominal health as the queue expands, because no task has missed its own slice. To detect this silent failure, verification experiments must isolate each stage of the causal chain: target appearance, optical capture, message dispatch, bus admission, lease expiration, and actuator torque onset. Without stage-by-stage timestamping, latency intervals are easily overlooked or double-counted.

Local watchdogs verify component execution, not end-to-end physical propagation.

Architect’s countermeasure: Enforce monotonic capture-to-torque evidence epoch timestamps across all processing nodes. Compare the sensor’s physical capture timestamp directly against the safety monitor’s decision tick, discarding any control proposal whose total elapsed latency exceeds the safety budget regardless of healthy local thread heartbeats.

Pitfall: Allowing diagnostic logging and high-throughput telemetry to share fieldbuses with real-time safety loops.

Real-time fieldbuses in physical AI (such as CAN FD and EtherCAT) are engineered for deterministic, low-latency control: EtherCAT cyclic Process Data Objects (PDOs) and CAN FD priority-arbitrated frames carry microsecond-accurate torque targets and lease-renewal heartbeats. When engineering teams attempt to capture rich diagnostic traces by multiplexing high-throughput telemetry (diagnostic core dumps, raw LiDAR point clouds, uncompressed camera debug streams) through Ethernet-over-EtherCAT (EoE) or bulk CAN FD payloads, they risk overflowing communication channels and starving safety-critical control loops.

On CAN FD networks, message arbitration is non-preemptive: once a low-priority diagnostic frame begins transmission on the physical layer, a newly ready high-priority emergency-stop or torque-command frame cannot preempt it. The resulting blocking latency depends on frame length, arbitration and data bit rates, and bit stuffing. Unregulated diagnostic bursts therefore inflate queueing jitter and exhaust hard timing budgets, even though every frame on the bus is structurally valid. Because CAN controllers do not register arbitration delays or link saturation as electrical errors, hardware error counters remain clean while safety deadlines expire. Similarly, while an EtherCAT master partitions cyclic process data from acyclic mailbox traffic, shared host network interface cards, protocol stack buffers, and slave microcontroller processing pipelines can still experience severe resource contention under heavy mailbox load. If mailbox servicing delays PDO packet processing or causes host-side scheduling jitter, safety heartbeats fail.

When unbudgeted telemetry delays a safety lease renewal or drive update past its deadline, the affected watchdog must invoke its qualified local fail-safe response. Furthermore, if a physical emergency-stop command travels across that same saturated bus, transmission delays directly consume stopping clearance. Verification trials must capture synchronized electrical communication traces alongside physical vehicle trajectories; observing a software timeout in isolation cannot prove that the machine arrested motion within its certified clearance envelope.

Unbudgeted diagnostic traffic can delay the commands and evidence needed for a bounded response.

Architect’s countermeasure: Physically isolate high-bandwidth diagnostic telemetry from real-time safety buses on separate network media, or enforce rigorous protocol-specific traffic shaping. On CAN FD, this means strict frame-length caps, reserved identifier priorities, and rate-limiting ingress filters. On EtherCAT, it means dedicated cyclic and mailbox schedule partitioning with strict execution time budgets. Ethernet Time-Sensitive Networking (TSN) scheduling and frame preemption standards (IEEE 802.1Qbv and 802.1Qbu) apply only to compliant Ethernet hardware; they do not provide preemption across CAN FD or standard EtherCAT links.

Pitfall: Relying on software anomaly detectors that share electrical power or failure modes with monitored actuators.

Actuator dynamics, including rapid motor acceleration, locked-rotor stall currents, and sharp regenerative braking transitions, cause severe electrical disturbances on shared power rails and analog ground references. Inverter switching produces steep inductive voltage transients (\(\InductiveVoltage = \PowerRailInductance\,d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\)), while wiring harnesses and internal battery impedance introduce substantial voltage drops that can pull unregulated bus voltages below the minimum dropout threshold of downstream DC-DC converters. Adversarial testing campaigns (such as fault category F4) inject calibrated rail transients to measure whether these disturbances propagate into supposedly isolated sensor and safety controller supplies.

When digital transducers monitoring actuator velocity or position share a power distribution board, ground plane, or non-isolated buck regulator with motor gate drivers, supply dips and ground bounce propagate directly into sensor microcontrollers. A common failure mode is a frozen peripheral register: the transducer continues transmitting cyclically valid bus frames whose measurement payload has stopped updating. System behavior depends on representation: if an encoder freezes its cumulative position counter, a downstream velocity estimator that computes numerical derivatives will incorrectly deduce that the motor has reached zero velocity. Conversely, if the sensor freezes an internally computed velocity register, it may continuously report a nominal cruising speed.

For example, if a frozen velocity register continues reporting 1.3 m/s while the motor drive erroneously executes a full-throttle acceleration command, the telemetry frames will carry valid cyclic checksums and plausible values even though they no longer reflect physical reality. A software anomaly detector that monitors only motor telemetry cannot identify the divergence without an independent physical motion witness, such as a secondary wheel encoder, an inertial measurement unit, or optical flow sensors. The adversarial verification suite must explicitly inject both the electrical transient and the resulting register freeze, using external ground-truth tracking to monitor vehicle kinematics through the commanded stop.

A power fault that disrupts an actuator can simultaneously blind the sensor tasked with detecting it.

Architect’s countermeasure: Power safety-critical transducers and monitor microcontrollers from galvanically isolated, held-up auxiliary supplies equipped with sufficient energy-storage capacitance to ride through worst-case bus transients. Transducers must be configured so that internal hardware faults cannot masquerade as plausible measurements. For analog transducers, pulling an output to 0 V fails this requirement whenever 0 V represents a valid physical reading (such as zero speed or minimum distance). Reliable systems employ a “live zero” architecture with out-of-band diagnostic voltage levels, patterned after the NAMUR NE 43 standard for industrial transmitters (NAMUR 2021). For digital fieldbuses, enforce end-to-end communication protections featuring monotonic rolling sequence counters, timestamp freshness checks, and payload CRCs to detect repeated or corrupted frames (AUTOSAR 2024). A frozen measurement register can still travel in a fresh, correctly sequenced packet, so detecting that failure also requires monitoring physical acquisition progress and cross-checking the reported state against an independent motion witness.

NAMUR. 2021. NE 43: Standardization of the Signal Level for the Failure Information of Digital Transmitters. NAMUR, User Association of Automation Technology in Process Industries.
AUTOSAR. 2024. E2E Protocol Specification. No. 849. AUTOSAR.

Fallacy: Treating an unobservable test outcome where diagnostic loggers dropped packets as evidence of safety.

High-severity fault injection, such as short-circuiting an inverter drive phase, opening a contactor under full load, or coupling high-voltage electromagnetic interference (EMI) onto fieldbus cabling, generates intense radiated and conducted electrical transients within the test cell. Ground bounce and high inductive kickback routinely couple into the external data acquisition (DAQ) harness, desynchronizing high-speed digitizer clocks, overflowing PCIe frame-grabber ring buffers, or crashing host logging daemons into kernel panics.

Meanwhile, the physical mobile base comes to rest: either its spring-applied mechanical brakes engaged, its drive back-drove the gearbox to a stall, or it drifted into the test cell’s mechanical end-stop buffer. In an undisciplined test campaign, observers look at the stopped machine and declare the run a “PASS” (“no crash occurred and the system reached rest”), while dismissing the missing telemetry as an irrelevant “instrumentation glitch” or repeating the trial until the logger manages to survive.

This practice introduces survivorship bias. The trials in which the logger crashes are often those with the most violent electrical transients, which are the conditions most likely to cause the safety microcontroller to hang in an exception handler without commanding deceleration. An unrecorded trial cannot reveal which mechanism stopped the base, and an operational warehouse aisle has no end-stop buffer. Under a rigorous qualification methodology, any test run lacking continuous, verifiable telemetry must be classified as UNOBSERVABLE and treated as an immediate test defect. The campaign must report the explicit count of each verdict alongside all untested configurations. Summary ratios such as \(\PassedTrialCount / \TotalExecutedTrialCount\) serve as test-suite execution statistics rather than empirical failure-probability estimates derived from valid, independently scored trials. Before repeating that condition, the team must establish why the witnesses failed and qualify evidence capture under the injected environment.

Discarding unobservable trials conceals the exact vulnerability under test through survivorship bias.

Architect’s countermeasure: Categorize any test run with dropped, incomplete, or corrupted evidence as UNOBSERVABLE, retaining it as an evidence deficit whose cause must be investigated. Report all four verdict counts. Credit a required trial toward a protective claim only when its required evidence is complete and verified; repeated observable runs do not erase missing evidence from earlier trials.

15.13 Summary

The rack-end stopping claim begins as a physical distance and timing budget. Adversarial verification transforms this kinematic budget into a concrete, testable engineering contract by declaring the operating envelope across which it must hold, including the coupled corners that one-variable-at-a-time sweeps miss. A five-part test contract (bounded claim, declared conditions, precommitted oracle, stated injection procedure, provenance) decides whether any trial counts as evidence. Within it, each postulated fault is built from a five-element chain: the negated nominal condition, the injection point, the expected detector, the bounded physical response, and an observable pass condition scored on an independent witness. For example, when the chunk policy stalls while its host processor continues transmitting nominal heartbeats, the verification harness evaluates powered deceleration onset and travel to rest referenced strictly from the timestamp of the last valid lease renewal. An automated oracle fixed before the trial classifies each run into one of four mutually exclusive outcomes: PASS, FAIL, INVALID, or UNOBSERVABLE. Test coverage is then reported dimension by dimension, and the campaign is judged by its weakest dimension rather than by one aggregate percentage.

Developing a comprehensive fault catalog requires combining multiple analytical perspectives: inverting nominal design assumptions, tracing causal failure paths forward through physical mechanisms, evaluating authority boundaries, identifying shared root causes that induce coupled failures, and modeling adversarial disturbances at physical transducers. Some failure modes bypass communication buses and hardware sensors entirely. An adversarial physical patch, an optical spoof, or a maliciously crafted task instruction steers the learned proposer directly, testing the boundary of the proposal subsystem. Because the deterministic permission path monitors physical safety invariants rather than the semantic origin of a proposal, it intervenes only when an actuator command threatens a measured envelope limit. Consequently, such trials score strictly against the physical oracle. If an adversarial proposal induces an erratic or sub-optimal action that nonetheless remains entirely within the safe physical envelope, the physical predicates may pass, while the verification harness records the wrong action as a task-level coverage deficit rather than crediting it as a passed proposer-injection case.

Verification evidence must be gathered on the qualification rung that faithfully models the underlying physical mechanism. A software simulation cannot sag a power rail it does not model, whereas formal methods can verify compact, deterministic safety filters under explicit premises without establishing a closed-loop guarantee for the running machine’s learned proposers. On physical test benches, hardware fault injectors operate directly at interface boundaries, while instrumentation towers record ground truth using independent clocks and isolated power supplies to prevent observer coupling. During falsification, Signal Temporal Logic provides signed robustness margins that direct search algorithms toward worst-case counterexamples. However, simulated margins cannot transfer directly to physical claims without applying a discrepancy bound justified over the claimed conditions. Similarly, importance sampling converts targeted failure searches into operational failure rates only when the sampling proposal distribution is explicitly declared, covers the entire failure domain, and applies proper likelihood weights. In rare-event analysis, extreme value models support safety claims only to the extent justified by their distributional assumptions. A counterexample refutes a safety claim, whereas the absence of one leaves the unsearched domain open rather than certified. Eight relations formalize this quantitative framework, and table 6 summarizes what each bounds or decides.

Key Takeaways: Adversarial verification and fault injection
  • A fault tests a physical claim: Derive the injection, detector, response, and independent witness from the stopping or contact bound. Successful task execution alone does not exercise the protective response.
  • The environment bounds the evidence: Select each qualification rung for the mechanism it exercises. Simulation, processor execution, electrical benches, and physical rigs contribute complementary evidence with different blind spots.
  • Search finds counterexamples, not universal passes: Signed temporal margins help prioritize disturbances near a violation. An unsuccessful search does not establish a global minimum, and a fitted tail does not prove a hard physical limit.
  • Precommitment preserves the meaning of a pass: Fix conditions and predicates before injection. Keep FAIL, INVALID, and UNOBSERVABLE distinct, because missing or invalid evidence cannot support the stopping claim.
  • Verification hands release bounded evidence: Bind each fault record to its claim, builds, conditions, and raw measurements. Coverage gaps and model assumptions remain part of the fault records, so release can restrict authority where evidence ends.

Under the fourth law, permission must be earned (principle 4), and adversarial verification produces much of the evidence that earns it. Each fault record names the claim it supports, the envelope point it covered, and the oracle fixed before the run. The authority that the release decision can grant therefore reaches exactly as far as the passing records and stops at every INVALID, UNOBSERVABLE, or untested cell. The F1 record of this chapter shows the rule in its plainest form. Its timing path, candidate envelope point, and witnesses are specified, while its loaded braking premise remains unqualified and its outcome field is empty. Until measured trials on a physical rig with independent instrumentation fill that field, the stopping claim it describes grants no authority at all.

15.14 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Deliberate fault injection originated in the dependability community of the 1980s, where researchers applied electrical transients, pin-level shorts, and radiation directly to working microprocessors to determine whether fault-tolerant architectures could detect and contain hardware errors. Arlat et al. (1990) formalized this methodology with the MESSALINE tool, structuring each verification campaign around four operational dimensions: the injected faults, the workload activating them, the readouts captured across system boundaries, and the dependability measures derived from observations. This four-part taxonomy serves as the foundational template for the fault records developed throughout this chapter. As systems scaled in complexity, fault injection transitioned from physical pin manipulation to software instrumentation. The comprehensive survey by Hsueh et al. (1997) framed the defining engineering trade-off: hardware-implemented injection manipulates internal signals and electrical states unreachable by software, whereas software-implemented injection offers programmatic repeatability and lower operational cost at the expense of ignoring low-level hardware faults. This trade-off reappears at every rung of the cyber-physical qualification ladder. Early practitioners also discovered that monitoring hooks and profiling code unavoidably perturb execution timing and cache states, a distortion Gait (1986) termed the probe effect. Because sub-millisecond timing shifts can destabilize physical feedback loops, high-assurance verification architectures isolate witness observers onto independent power supplies and dedicated clock domains.

Hsueh, Mei-Chen, Timothy K. Tsai, and Ravishankar K. Iyer. 1997. “Fault Injection Techniques and Tools.” Computer 30 (4): 75–82. https://doi.org/10.1109/2.585157.
Pnueli, Amir. 1977. “The Temporal Logic of Programs.” 18th Annual Symposium on Foundations of Computer Science (SFCS), 46–57. https://doi.org/10.1109/SFCS.1977.32.
Maler, Oded, and Dejan Nickovic. 2004. “Monitoring Temporal Properties of Continuous Signals.” Formal Techniques, Modelling and Analysis of Timed and Fault-Tolerant Systems (FORMATS/FTRTFT), Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 3253: 152–66. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-540-30206-3_12.
Annpureddy, Yashwanth, Che Liu, Georgios Fainekos, and Sriram Sankaranarayanan. 2011. “S-TaLiRo: A Tool for Temporal Logic Falsification for Hybrid Systems.” International Conference on Tools and Algorithms for the Construction and Analysis of Systems (TACAS), Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 6605: 254–57. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-19835-9_21.
Donzé, Alexandre. 2010. “Breach, a Toolbox for Verification and Parameter Synthesis of Hybrid Systems.” Computer Aided Verification (CAV), Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 6174: 167–70. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-14295-6_17.
Rubinstein, Reuven Y., and Dirk P. Kroese. 2004. The Cross-Entropy Method: A Unified Approach to Combinatorial Optimization, Monte-Carlo Simulation and Machine Learning. Information Science and Statistics. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4757-4321-0.
O’Kelly, Matthew, Aman Sinha, Hongseok Namkoong, John Duchi, and Russ Tedrake. 2018. “Scalable End-to-End Autonomous Vehicle Testing via Rare-Event Simulation.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 31.
Balkema, August A., and Laurens de Haan. 1974. “Residual Life Time at Great Age.” The Annals of Probability 2 (5): 792–804. https://doi.org/10.1214/aop/1176996548.
Pickands, James, III. 1975. “Statistical Inference Using Extreme Order Statistics.” The Annals of Statistics 3 (1): 119–31. https://doi.org/10.1214/aos/1176343003.

Search-based falsification and continuous verification followed a parallel lineage rooted in formal methods and statistical physics. Pnueli (1977) first introduced linear temporal logic to prove correctness properties over discrete program executions. To verify continuous and hybrid physical trajectories, Maler and Nickovic (2004) generalized temporal assertions into signal temporal logic (STL). Fainekos and Pappas (2009) subsequently equipped STL with quantitative spatial semantics, replacing binary true-false verdicts with a continuous signed distance margin. This formulation transformed the falsification problem from an intractable combinatorial search into continuous gradient-free or gradient-based optimization. Cyber-physical falsification frameworks such as S-TaLiRo (Annpureddy et al. 2011) and Breach (Donzé 2010) packaged these continuous margins to automate the discovery of safety violations in nonlinear dynamical models. Statistical rare-event estimation has an even older heritage. Importance sampling emerged from early Monte Carlo radiation shielding calculations by Kahn and Marshall (1953), the cross-entropy method introduced adaptive proposal distributions (Rubinstein and Kroese 2004), and O’Kelly et al. (2018) synthesized both techniques to quantify catastrophic collision probabilities across autonomous driving stacks. When safety analysts must characterize worst-case physical excursions without imposing restrictive global distribution assumptions, they draw on extreme value theory. It entered engineering through the flood-frequency analyses of Gumbel (1958) and was rigorously established in its modern peaks-over-threshold form by Balkema and Haan (1974) and Pickands (1975).

Formal verification of learned neural controllers is the most recent strand. Reluplex (Katz et al. 2017) proved that modified simplex constraint solvers could formally verify input-output safety properties on small piecewise-linear networks. Subsequent bound-propagation frameworks, including α,β-CROWN (Wang et al. 2021), scaled formal guarantees to million-parameter vision networks using branch-and-bound techniques and convex relaxation. However, formal solvers remain computationally bounded; the cited results do not establish guarantees for multi-billion-parameter vision-language-action foundation models operating in closed feedback loops. The verification architecture developed in this chapter unifies these historical lineages into a cohesive systems discipline. Each trial executes against an explicit operational claim, evaluates trajectories with an oracle declared before the run, isolates observational telemetry within uncorrupted witness hardware, and exports fault records that carry measured coverage deficits into deployment safety cases.

Katz, Guy, Clark Barrett, David L. Dill, Kyle Julian, and Mykel J. Kochenderfer. 2017. “Reluplex: An Efficient SMT Solver for Verifying Deep Neural Networks.” International Conference on Computer Aided Verification (CAV), 97–117. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-63387-9_5.
Wang, Shiqi, Huan Zhang, Kaidi Xu, Xue Lin, Suman Jana, Cho-Jui Hsieh, and J. Zico Kolter. 2021. “Beta-CROWN: Efficient Bound Propagation with Per-Neuron Split Constraints for Neural Network Robustness Verification.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 34.

Six foundational works trace the trajectory from classical hardware dependability to modern cyber-physical validation algorithms:

  • Arlat et al. (1990) establishes fault injection as a controlled experimental science, defining the four-factor schema of faults, activation workloads, readouts, and derived measures that governs the fault records in section 15.4.2.
  • Leveson (2011) introduces Systems-Theoretic Accident Model and Processes (STAMP) and System-Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA), establishing the forward hazard methodology in section 15.6.2 that identifies systemic safety violations emerging from unsafe control interactions rather than isolated component failures.
  • Fainekos and Pappas (2009) formulates the continuous spatial robustness degree for temporal logic specifications over real-valued signals, providing the signed optimization margin evaluated throughout section 15.9.1.
  • Owen (2013) provides a definitive mathematical foundation for importance sampling and rare-event estimation, detailing effective sample size diagnostics, weight degeneracies, and failure modes where unrepresentative proposals yield falsely optimistic variance estimates.
  • Corso et al. (2021) surveys algorithmic safety validation for black-box autonomous systems, categorizing falsification approaches according to whether they target counterexample discovery, most-likely-failure trajectories, or rare failure probabilities.
  • Kochenderfer et al. (2026) synthesizes trajectory falsification, failure-probability estimation, reachable set analysis, and runtime monitoring into an algorithmic discipline for validating complex autonomous systems.
Arlat, Jean, Martine Aguera, Louis Amat, Yves Crouzet, Jean-Charles Fabre, Jean-Claude Laprie, Eliane Martins, and David Powell. 1990. “Fault Injection for Dependability Validation: A Methodology and Some Applications.” IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering 16 (2): 166–82. https://doi.org/10.1109/32.44380.
Fainekos, Georgios E., and George J. Pappas. 2009. “Robustness of Temporal Logic Specifications for Continuous-Time Signals.” Theoretical Computer Science 410 (42): 4262–91. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.tcs.2009.06.021.
Owen, Art B. 2013. Monte Carlo Theory, Methods and Examples. Online book.
Corso, Anthony, Robert Moss, Mark Koren, Ritchie Lee, and Mykel J. Kochenderfer. 2021. “A Survey of Algorithms for Black-Box Safety Validation of Cyber-Physical Systems.” Journal of Artificial Intelligence Research 72. https://doi.org/10.1613/jair.1.12716.
Kochenderfer, Mykel J., Sydney M. Katz, Anthony L. Corso, and Robert J. Moss. 2026. Algorithms for Validation. MIT Press.

What’s Next: From verification to release decisions
Verification delivers to the release gate a signed set of fault records, the per-rung coverage of the qualification ladder behind them, and an explicit account of the operating regimes no trial reached. For each safety claim, the set binds the corresponding fault records to any sim-to-real transfer bound (\(\TransferDiscrepancyBound\)) or rare-event tail estimate on which the claim depends, keeping measured margins, conditional model estimates, and untested regimes strictly apart. Verification records supply behavioral evidence under fault conditions, but they do not make deployment policy, and no individual fault record decides whether the machine may operate in production aisles. In Deployment Release, the release process writes each claim as a physical inequality and joins these fault records with the authority record, the placement record, and the evaluation record in a safety case. It then judges which claims are substantiated, which release verdict applies (UNCOND, COND, or REFUSE), and which standing conditions must govern live operation.
